**LAB 01**  
**Week 1  |  Module 1: AI & ML Foundations Recap \+ Technical Setup**

**Environment Setup: Colab, Python Refresher & Libraries**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Set up a reproducible Python environment in Google Colab, refresh core Python syntax, and install and import the essential AI/ML libraries used throughout Level 2\.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Level 2 is hands-on and technical. Every applied AI task — machine learning, data handling, API calls, RAG, agents — runs on Python and a stack of specialised libraries. A correctly configured environment is the foundation; time spent setting it up properly saves hours of debugging later.

Google Colab remains the recommended environment: it is free, runs in the browser, provides a GPU option, and comes with most data-science libraries pre-installed. Code runs in cells, outputs appear inline, and notebooks can be saved to Google Drive. For libraries not pre-installed, pip installs them in seconds.

The core Level 2 stack includes: numpy (numerical arrays), pandas (tabular data), matplotlib (plotting), and scikit-learn (machine learning). Later modules add openai/anthropic clients, langchain, and vector-database libraries. Importing them with standard aliases (np, pd, plt) is a universal convention.

Reproducibility matters in real projects. Pinning library versions, setting random seeds, and structuring a notebook clearly (setup cell, then logic) ensures results can be repeated. This professional habit distinguishes reliable AI work from one-off experiments that cannot be reproduced.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab (free, browser-based) |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Language** | Python 3.x |
| **Libraries** | numpy, pandas, matplotlib, scikit-learn |
| **Deliverable** | A verified setup notebook |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Open Google Colab and create a new notebook named 'L2\_Setup'.

2. In the first cell, import numpy, pandas, and matplotlib with standard aliases.

3. Print each library's version to confirm installation.

4. Install any missing library with pip (e.g. a specific scikit-learn version).

5. Set a random seed for reproducibility.

6. Run a small Python refresher: a function, a loop, and a list comprehension.

7. Save the notebook to Google Drive.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# L2 Setup & Python Refresher (Google Colab)   \# \--- Imports with standard aliases \--- import numpy as np import pandas as pd import matplotlib.pyplot as plt import sklearn   \# \--- Verify versions (reproducibility) \--- print("numpy      :", np.\_\_version\_\_) print("pandas     :", pd.\_\_version\_\_) print("scikit-learn:", sklearn.\_\_version\_\_)   \# \--- Install a missing/pinned library if needed \--- \# \!pip install scikit-learn==1.5.0 \-q   \# \--- Set a random seed so results are repeatable \--- import random random.seed(42) np.random.seed(42)   \# \--- Quick Python refresher \--- def squared(nums):     """Return a list of squares using a comprehension."""     return \[n \*\* 2 for n in nums\]   data \= \[1, 2, 3, 4, 5\] print("Squares:", squared(data))   \# Dictionary \+ loop record \= {"model": "logreg", "accuracy": 0.91} for key, value in record.items():     print(f"{key}: {value}") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A Colab notebook that imports the core libraries, prints their versions, sets a random seed, and runs a short Python refresher (function, comprehension, dictionary loop) with all cells executing without error.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Create a fresh Colab notebook, import the four core libraries, print their versions, and run a short Python refresher with a function and a list comprehension.

2. Task 2: Write a function that takes a list of numbers and returns a dictionary with their min, max, and average.

3. Task 3: Install one additional library of your choice with pip and import it successfully.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. Why is Google Colab well-suited to AI work?**

**Answer:** It is free, browser-based, offers a GPU option, and comes with most data-science libraries pre-installed, with pip for the rest.

**Q2. What are the four core Level 2 libraries?**

**Answer:** numpy for numerical arrays, pandas for tabular data, matplotlib for plotting, and scikit-learn for machine learning.

**Q3. Why set a random seed?**

**Answer:** To make results reproducible, so the same code produces the same output each time it runs.

**Q4. What are the standard aliases for numpy and pandas?**

**Answer:** np for numpy and pd for pandas, a universal convention.

**Q5. Why pin library versions in a project?**

**Answer:** To ensure reproducibility, so the code behaves consistently even as libraries are updated.

**LAB 02**  
**Week 1  |  Module 1: AI & ML Foundations Recap \+ Technical Setup**

**ML Foundations Recap: Supervised vs Unsupervised Learning**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Recap the core machine learning paradigms — supervised and unsupervised learning — and map common algorithms and real tasks to each.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Machine Learning is the engine of applied AI. Before building models, you must understand the two foundational paradigms: supervised and unsupervised learning. The distinction determines which algorithms apply and what kind of data is needed.

Supervised learning trains on labelled data — examples paired with correct answers. It splits into classification (predicting a category, e.g. spam or not-spam) and regression (predicting a number, e.g. house price). The model learns the mapping from inputs to known outputs, then predicts on new data.

Unsupervised learning works on unlabelled data, finding structure without given answers. Its main tasks are clustering (grouping similar items, e.g. customer segments) and dimensionality reduction (simplifying data while keeping its essence). It discovers patterns rather than predicting a target.

Each paradigm maps to common algorithms: supervised includes linear/logistic regression, decision trees, and k-nearest neighbours; unsupervised includes k-means clustering and PCA. Recognising which paradigm a problem needs — do we have labels and a target to predict? — is the first decision in any ML project.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Colab \+ document editor |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Supervised vs unsupervised paradigms |
| **Deliverable** | Paradigm-mapping reference |
| **Activity** | Classify problems by paradigm |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Write definitions of supervised and unsupervised learning.

2. Split supervised into classification and regression with an example each.

3. Split unsupervised into clustering and dimensionality reduction with examples.

4. List two algorithms under each paradigm.

5. Take five real problems and classify each by paradigm.

6. Note what data each problem would require.

7. Compile a one-page mapping reference.

**▍ Paradigm Map & Classification**

| \# ML Paradigm Map (conceptual reference)   SUPERVISED (labelled data, predict a target) ├── Classification \-\> predict a CATEGORY │     e.g. spam/not-spam, disease/no-disease │     algos: Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, KNN └── Regression \-\> predict a NUMBER       e.g. house price, temperature       algos: Linear Regression, Random Forest   UNSUPERVISED (no labels, find structure) ├── Clustering \-\> GROUP similar items │     e.g. customer segments │     algo: K-Means └── Dimensionality Reduction \-\> SIMPLIFY data       e.g. compress features       algo: PCA   \# Decision question for any problem: \# "Do I have labelled data AND a target to predict?" \#   Yes \-\> Supervised   |   No \-\> Unsupervised   problems \= \[     ("Predict if email is spam", "Supervised / Classification"),     ("Group customers by behaviour", "Unsupervised / Clustering"),     ("Predict house price", "Supervised / Regression"), \] for p, kind in problems:     print(f"{p:\<32} \-\> {kind}") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A one-page reference mapping supervised and unsupervised learning to their sub-tasks and algorithms, plus five real problems each correctly classified by paradigm with the required data noted.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Classify five real-world problems by ML paradigm (supervised/classification, supervised/regression, or unsupervised/clustering) and justify each.

2. Task 2: For one supervised and one unsupervised problem, describe exactly what the training data would look like.

3. Task 3: Name a real product you use and identify which ML paradigm likely powers it.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is supervised learning?**

**Answer:** Training on labelled data — examples paired with correct answers — to learn a mapping from inputs to outputs, then predict on new data.

**Q2. What is the difference between classification and regression?**

**Answer:** Classification predicts a category, while regression predicts a continuous number.

**Q3. What is unsupervised learning?**

**Answer:** Learning from unlabelled data to find structure, such as clustering similar items or reducing dimensionality, without a given target.

**Q4. Name one algorithm for clustering.**

**Answer:** K-Means, which groups data into clusters of similar items.

**Q5. What is the first decision in an ML project?**

**Answer:** Whether you have labelled data and a target to predict, which determines if the problem is supervised or unsupervised.

**LAB 03**  
**Week 1  |  Module 1: AI & ML Foundations Recap \+ Technical Setup**

**Loading & Exploring Data with Pandas**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Load a real dataset with pandas and perform exploratory data analysis (EDA) — inspecting shape, types, summary statistics, and missing values.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Every ML project begins with understanding the data. pandas is the standard Python library for loading and exploring tabular data. Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) is the systematic first look that reveals a dataset's structure, quality, and quirks before any modelling.

pandas represents data as a DataFrame — a labelled table of rows and columns. Data loads from CSV, Excel, or URLs with a single function like pd.read\_csv(). Once loaded, methods like .head(), .shape, .info(), and .describe() give an immediate overview of the data.

Key EDA steps include: checking the number of rows and columns (.shape), inspecting column types and non-null counts (.info()), viewing summary statistics for numeric columns (.describe()), and counting missing values (.isnull().sum()). Together these answer 'what is in this data and is it clean?'

EDA guides every later decision. Spotting missing values informs cleaning; seeing data types informs preprocessing; noticing skew or outliers informs modelling choices. Skilled practitioners never model blind — they explore first, because understanding the data prevents costly mistakes downstream.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | pandas |
| **Deliverable** | An EDA notebook on a real dataset |
| **Dataset** | A public CSV (e.g. Titanic/Iris) |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Load a public dataset into a pandas DataFrame.

2. View the first rows with .head().

3. Check the dataset's shape (rows, columns).

4. Inspect column types and non-null counts with .info().

5. Generate summary statistics with .describe().

6. Count missing values per column.

7. Write three observations about the data.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Exploratory Data Analysis with pandas (Colab)   import pandas as pd   \# \--- Load a dataset (example: a public CSV by URL) \--- url \= "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/datasciencedojo/datasets/master/titanic.csv" df \= pd.read\_csv(url)   \# \--- First look \--- print("Shape (rows, cols):", df.shape) df.head()          \# shows first 5 rows in Colab   \# \--- Column types and non-null counts \--- df.info()   \# \--- Summary statistics for numeric columns \--- print(df.describe())   \# \--- Missing values per column \--- print("\\nMissing values:") print(df.isnull().sum())   \# \--- A couple of quick explorations \--- print("\\nSurvival counts:") print(df\["Survived"\].value\_counts())   print("\\nAverage age:", round(df\["Age"\].mean(), 1)) |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

An EDA notebook that loads a real dataset and reports its shape, column types, summary statistics, and missing-value counts, ending with three written observations about the data.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Load a real dataset and produce an EDA summary: shape, column types, summary statistics, and a count of missing values.

2. Task 2: Identify the column with the most missing values and propose how you would handle it.

3. Task 3: Use value\_counts() on a categorical column and describe what the distribution tells you.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is a pandas DataFrame?**

**Answer:** A labelled table of rows and columns, the standard structure for tabular data in pandas.

**Q2. What is Exploratory Data Analysis?**

**Answer:** The systematic first look at a dataset that reveals its structure, quality, and quirks before modelling.

**Q3. What does df.describe() show?**

**Answer:** Summary statistics for numeric columns, such as count, mean, standard deviation, min, max, and quartiles.

**Q4. How do you count missing values in a DataFrame?**

**Answer:** With df.isnull().sum(), which counts the nulls in each column.

**Q5. Why explore data before modelling?**

**Answer:** Because understanding the data's structure and quality prevents costly mistakes and guides cleaning and modelling decisions.

**LAB 04**  
**Week 2  |  Module 1: AI & ML Foundations Recap \+ Technical Setup**

**Data Cleaning & Preprocessing with Pandas**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Clean a real dataset by handling missing values, removing duplicates, fixing data types, and encoding categorical variables for modelling.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Raw data is rarely model-ready. Data cleaning and preprocessing transform messy data into a clean, numeric form that algorithms can use. This stage often consumes most of a project's time and has a large impact on model quality — 'garbage in, garbage out' is the rule.

Handling missing values is a core task. Options include dropping rows or columns (.dropna()), or filling gaps (.fillna()) with a sensible value such as the mean, median, or mode. The choice depends on how much data is missing and why. Removing duplicates with .drop\_duplicates() prevents skewed results.

Data types must be correct. Numbers stored as text cannot be computed; dates may need parsing. pandas methods like .astype() convert types. Standardising text (consistent capitalisation, trimming whitespace) resolves inconsistencies that would otherwise fragment categories.

Categorical variables must become numeric for most algorithms. Encoding techniques include one-hot encoding (a column per category) and label encoding (an integer per category). Choosing the right encoding matters — one-hot avoids implying false order among categories, which label encoding can introduce.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | pandas |
| **Deliverable** | A cleaned, model-ready dataset |
| **Techniques** | fillna, dropna, encoding |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Load a dataset with known quality issues.

2. Count and inspect missing values.

3. Fill or drop missing values with justification.

4. Remove any duplicate rows.

5. Fix incorrect data types.

6. Encode a categorical column (one-hot or label).

7. Confirm the cleaned dataset is fully numeric where needed.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Data Cleaning & Preprocessing (Colab)   import pandas as pd   df \= pd.read\_csv("data.csv")   \# your dataset   \# \--- 1\. Inspect missing values \--- print(df.isnull().sum())   \# \--- 2\. Handle missing values \--- \# Fill numeric column with the median (robust to outliers) df\["Age"\] \= df\["Age"\].fillna(df\["Age"\].median()) \# Fill categorical column with the mode df\["City"\] \= df\["City"\].fillna(df\["City"\].mode()\[0\]) \# Drop rows still missing a critical field df \= df.dropna(subset=\["Target"\])   \# \--- 3\. Remove duplicates \--- df \= df.drop\_duplicates()   \# \--- 4\. Fix data types \--- df\["Price"\] \= df\["Price"\].astype(float)   \# \--- 5\. Standardise text \--- df\["City"\] \= df\["City"\].str.strip().str.title()   \# \--- 6\. Encode categorical variables \--- \# One-hot encoding (no false ordering) df \= pd.get\_dummies(df, columns=\["City"\], drop\_first=True)   print("Cleaned shape:", df.shape) df.head() |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A cleaned dataset where missing values are handled, duplicates removed, types corrected, text standardised, and categorical variables encoded — confirmed model-ready by inspecting the final DataFrame.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Take a messy dataset and clean it: handle missing values, remove duplicates, fix types, and encode at least one categorical column.

2. Task 2: Compare dropping rows with missing values versus filling them, and explain which is better for your dataset and why.

3. Task 3: Apply both one-hot and label encoding to a categorical column and explain when each is appropriate.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. Why is data cleaning important for ML?**

**Answer:** Because algorithms produce poor results on messy data — 'garbage in, garbage out' — so clean, correct data is essential.

**Q2. Name two ways to handle missing values.**

**Answer:** Dropping rows or columns with dropna(), or filling gaps with fillna() using the mean, median, or mode.

**Q3. Why must categorical variables be encoded?**

**Answer:** Because most algorithms require numeric input, so categories must be converted to numbers.

**Q4. What is the advantage of one-hot encoding over label encoding?**

**Answer:** One-hot avoids implying a false order among categories, which label encoding can mistakenly introduce.

**Q5. How do you remove duplicate rows in pandas?**

**Answer:** With df.drop\_duplicates(), which removes repeated records that would otherwise skew results.

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Create informative visualizations with matplotlib — histograms, bar charts, scatter plots, and correlation views — to uncover patterns in data.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Visualization turns numbers into understanding. matplotlib is Python's foundational plotting library, capable of producing the full range of charts needed for data analysis. Good visualizations reveal distributions, relationships, and anomalies that summary statistics alone can hide.

Different charts answer different questions. Histograms show the distribution of a single numeric variable; bar charts compare categories; scatter plots reveal relationships between two numeric variables; and line charts show trends over time. Choosing the right chart for the question is a core skill.

matplotlib follows a consistent pattern: create a figure, plot the data, then label axes, add a title, and display. plt.hist(), plt.bar(), plt.scatter(), and plt.plot() cover most needs. Clear labels and titles are not optional — an unlabelled chart cannot communicate its insight.

Beyond single charts, exploring relationships matters. A correlation heatmap (via a column correlation matrix) shows how numeric features relate to each other and to the target — invaluable for feature selection. Visual EDA complements numeric EDA, together giving a full picture before modelling.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Libraries** | matplotlib, pandas |
| **Deliverable** | A multi-chart EDA visual report |
| **Charts** | Histogram, bar, scatter, correlation |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Load a dataset into pandas.

2. Plot a histogram of one numeric column.

3. Plot a bar chart comparing a categorical column's counts.

4. Plot a scatter chart of two numeric columns.

5. Compute and display a correlation matrix.

6. Label every chart with axes and a title.

7. Write one insight per chart.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Data Visualization with matplotlib (Colab)   import pandas as pd import matplotlib.pyplot as plt   df \= pd.read\_csv("data.csv")   \# \--- Histogram: distribution of a numeric column \--- plt.figure(figsize=(6, 4)) plt.hist(df\["Age"\].dropna(), bins=20, color="\#0E7C86", edgecolor="white") plt.title("Age Distribution") plt.xlabel("Age") plt.ylabel("Count") plt.show()   \# \--- Bar chart: counts per category \--- counts \= df\["City"\].value\_counts() plt.figure(figsize=(6, 4)) plt.bar(counts.index, counts.values, color="\#E8743B") plt.title("Records per City") plt.xlabel("City") plt.ylabel("Count") plt.xticks(rotation=45) plt.show()   \# \--- Scatter: relationship between two numeric variables \--- plt.figure(figsize=(6, 4)) plt.scatter(df\["Age"\], df\["Fare"\], alpha=0.5) plt.title("Age vs Fare") plt.xlabel("Age") plt.ylabel("Fare") plt.show()   \# \--- Correlation matrix (numeric columns) \--- corr \= df.corr(numeric\_only=True) print(corr) |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A visual EDA report containing a labelled histogram, bar chart, and scatter plot plus a correlation matrix, each accompanied by a written insight describing what the chart reveals.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Create a visual EDA report with at least three labelled charts (histogram, bar, scatter) and write one insight per chart.

2. Task 2: Compute a correlation matrix and identify the two features most strongly related to the target.

3. Task 3: Take a poorly labelled chart and improve it with a clear title, axis labels, and sensible colours.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What does a histogram show?**

**Answer:** The distribution of a single numeric variable — how values are spread across ranges.

**Q2. Which chart reveals the relationship between two numeric variables?**

**Answer:** A scatter plot, which shows how one variable relates to another.

**Q3. What is the standard matplotlib plotting pattern?**

**Answer:** Create a figure, plot the data, then add axis labels, a title, and display the chart.

**Q4. Why is a correlation matrix useful before modelling?**

**Answer:** It shows how features relate to each other and to the target, helping with feature selection.

**Q5. Why must charts be labelled?**

**Answer:** Because an unlabelled chart cannot communicate its insight; titles and axis labels make the meaning clear.

**LAB 06**  
**Week 2  |  Module 1: AI & ML Foundations Recap \+ Technical Setup**

**Feature Engineering & Train/Test Split**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Engineer useful features from raw data and correctly split a dataset into training and test sets to prepare for model building.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Models are only as good as their features. Feature engineering is the craft of creating, transforming, and selecting input variables that help a model learn. Well-engineered features often improve performance more than switching algorithms — it is where domain insight meets data.

Common feature-engineering techniques include: creating new features from existing ones (e.g. extracting 'title' from a name, or 'age group' from age), scaling numeric features to a common range, and combining or transforming columns. The goal is to expose patterns the model can use.

Before training, data must be split into a training set (to learn from) and a test set (to evaluate on). The train/test split — typically 70-80% train, 20-30% test — is essential to measure how well a model generalises to unseen data. scikit-learn's train\_test\_split does this cleanly.

A critical principle is avoiding data leakage: the test set must never influence training. Feature engineering decisions and scaling parameters should be derived from the training data only, then applied to the test data. Violating this gives falsely optimistic results that collapse in the real world.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | scikit-learn, pandas |
| **Deliverable** | Engineered features \+ clean split |
| **Function** | train\_test\_split |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Load a cleaned dataset.

2. Engineer one new feature from existing columns.

3. Scale or transform a numeric feature if useful.

4. Separate the features (X) from the target (y).

5. Split into training and test sets with train\_test\_split.

6. Confirm the split sizes and that the target is balanced.

7. Note why the test set must stay untouched during training.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Feature Engineering & Train/Test Split (Colab)   import pandas as pd from sklearn.model\_selection import train\_test\_split from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler   df \= pd.read\_csv("clean\_data.csv")   \# \--- Feature engineering: create a new feature \--- \# Example: family size from siblings \+ parents \+ self df\["FamilySize"\] \= df\["SibSp"\] \+ df\["Parch"\] \+ 1 \# Example: age group buckets df\["AgeGroup"\] \= pd.cut(df\["Age"\], bins=\[0, 12, 18, 60, 100\],                         labels=\["child", "teen", "adult", "senior"\]) df \= pd.get\_dummies(df, columns=\["AgeGroup"\], drop\_first=True)   \# \--- Separate features (X) and target (y) \--- y \= df\["Survived"\] X \= df.drop(columns=\["Survived"\])   \# \--- Train/test split (80/20), stratified on the target \--- X\_train, X\_test, y\_train, y\_test \= train\_test\_split(     X, y, test\_size=0.2, random\_state=42, stratify=y )   print("Train size:", X\_train.shape) print("Test size :", X\_test.shape)   \# \--- Scale numeric features using TRAIN stats only (no leakage) \--- scaler \= StandardScaler() num\_cols \= \["Age", "Fare", "FamilySize"\] X\_train\[num\_cols\] \= scaler.fit\_transform(X\_train\[num\_cols\]) X\_test\[num\_cols\] \= scaler.transform(X\_test\[num\_cols\])  \# transform only |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A prepared dataset with at least one engineered feature, separated X and y, a stratified train/test split with confirmed sizes, and numeric features scaled using training statistics only to avoid leakage.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Engineer at least one new feature from a dataset and perform a correct train/test split, confirming the split sizes.

2. Task 2: Explain what data leakage is and show how scaling on the full dataset would cause it.

3. Task 3: Create an 'age group' or similar bucketed feature and justify why it might help the model.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is feature engineering?**

**Answer:** Creating, transforming, and selecting input variables that help a model learn, often improving performance more than changing algorithms.

**Q2. Why split data into training and test sets?**

**Answer:** To measure how well the model generalises to unseen data, rather than just memorising the training set.

**Q3. What is a typical train/test split ratio?**

**Answer:** Around 70-80% for training and 20-30% for testing.

**Q4. What is data leakage?**

**Answer:** When information from the test set influences training, producing falsely optimistic results that fail in the real world.

**Q5. How do you avoid leakage when scaling?**

**Answer:** Fit the scaler on the training data only, then apply (transform) it to the test data.

**LAB 07**  
**Week 3  |  Module 2: Ethics & Responsible AI in Practice**

**Detecting Bias in Datasets & Model Predictions**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Detect and measure bias in a dataset and in model predictions, applying fairness concepts to real outputs.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Responsible AI begins with seeing bias clearly. Bias in machine learning means systematic unfairness in data or predictions that disadvantages certain groups. In Level 2 this becomes concrete: you measure bias in actual datasets and model outputs rather than discussing it abstractly.

Bias often originates in data. A dataset may under-represent a group, contain historically biased labels, or encode proxies for sensitive attributes. Detecting it starts with examining group distributions — how outcomes differ across categories like gender or region — using pandas group-by analysis.

Bias also appears in predictions. A model trained on biased data can produce unequal outcomes — for example, approving one group at a higher rate than another with equal qualifications. Measuring this requires comparing prediction rates and error rates across groups, not just overall accuracy.

Simple fairness metrics make bias measurable: demographic parity (do groups receive positive outcomes at similar rates?) and equal accuracy across groups. While full fairness auditing is advanced, computing group-wise outcome rates is an accessible, powerful first step that surfaces real disparities.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | pandas, scikit-learn |
| **Deliverable** | A bias audit of group outcomes |
| **Metric** | Group-wise outcome rates |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Load a dataset that includes a sensitive attribute (e.g. gender).

2. Compute the overall positive-outcome rate.

3. Group by the sensitive attribute and compare outcome rates.

4. Train a simple model and generate predictions.

5. Compare the model's prediction rates across groups.

6. Identify any disparity.

7. Summarise the bias findings.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Detecting Bias in Data & Predictions (Colab)   import pandas as pd from sklearn.linear\_model import LogisticRegression from sklearn.model\_selection import train\_test\_split   df \= pd.read\_csv("loans.csv")   \# includes 'Gender' and 'Approved'   \# \--- 1\. Bias in the DATA: outcome rates by group \--- print("Overall approval rate:", round(df\["Approved"\].mean(), 3)) print("\\nApproval rate by gender:") print(df.groupby("Gender")\["Approved"\].mean())   \# \--- 2\. Train a simple model \--- X \= pd.get\_dummies(df.drop(columns=\["Approved"\]), drop\_first=True) y \= df\["Approved"\] X\_tr, X\_te, y\_tr, y\_te, g\_tr, g\_te \= train\_test\_split(     X, y, df\["Gender"\], test\_size=0.3, random\_state=42)   model \= LogisticRegression(max\_iter=1000).fit(X\_tr, y\_tr) preds \= model.predict(X\_te)   \# \--- 3\. Bias in PREDICTIONS: approval rate by group \--- audit \= pd.DataFrame({"gender": g\_te.values, "pred": preds}) print("\\nModel approval rate by gender:") print(audit.groupby("gender")\["pred"\].mean())   \# Disparity \= difference between group rates rates \= audit.groupby("gender")\["pred"\].mean() print("\\nDisparity:", round(abs(rates.iloc\[0\] \- rates.iloc\[1\]), 3)) |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A bias audit comparing positive-outcome rates across a sensitive group in both the raw data and a model's predictions, with the disparity quantified and findings summarised.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Audit a dataset and a model's predictions for bias by computing and comparing group-wise outcome rates, then quantify the disparity.

2. Task 2: Propose two concrete steps that could reduce the disparity you found.

3. Task 3: Explain why high overall accuracy does not guarantee a fair model.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is bias in machine learning?**

**Answer:** Systematic unfairness in data or predictions that disadvantages certain groups.

**Q2. Where does bias in ML often originate?**

**Answer:** In the data — through under-representation, historically biased labels, or proxies for sensitive attributes.

**Q3. How can you detect bias in predictions?**

**Answer:** By comparing prediction rates and error rates across groups, not just overall accuracy.

**Q4. What is demographic parity?**

**Answer:** A fairness notion asking whether different groups receive positive outcomes at similar rates.

**Q5. Why is overall accuracy insufficient for fairness?**

**Answer:** Because a model can be accurate overall while producing unequal outcomes or error rates across groups.

**LAB 08**  
**Week 3  |  Module 2: Ethics & Responsible AI in Practice**

**Bias Mitigation Techniques & Fairness Toolkits**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Apply basic bias mitigation techniques and explore fairness toolkit concepts to reduce disparities in model outcomes.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Detecting bias is only useful if it can be reduced. Bias mitigation covers techniques applied before, during, or after training to make outcomes fairer. Even simple interventions can meaningfully reduce disparity, and understanding the categories of approaches guides responsible practice.

Mitigation techniques fall into three stages. Pre-processing adjusts the data (re-balancing groups, reweighting samples, removing biased features). In-processing modifies the learning algorithm to penalise unfairness. Post-processing adjusts predictions after training (e.g. tuning decision thresholds per group to equalise outcomes).

Practical, accessible techniques include: re-balancing an under-represented group through resampling, removing or masking a sensitive attribute and its obvious proxies, and adjusting the classification threshold to bring group outcome rates closer together. Each involves trade-offs between fairness and accuracy.

Fairness toolkits such as AI Fairness 360 and Fairlearn package these techniques and provide standardised metrics. While their advanced use is beyond this lab, knowing they exist — and understanding the pre/in/post-processing framework — prepares you to apply fairness systematically in real projects.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | scikit-learn, pandas |
| **Deliverable** | Before/after fairness comparison |
| **Concept** | Pre/in/post-processing mitigation |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Start from the biased model and audit from the previous session.

2. Choose a mitigation technique (e.g. resampling or threshold adjustment).

3. Apply the technique to the data or predictions.

4. Re-train or re-score the model.

5. Re-measure the group-wise outcome rates.

6. Compare disparity before and after.

7. Note the fairness-accuracy trade-off observed.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Bias Mitigation: threshold adjustment (Colab)   import pandas as pd import numpy as np from sklearn.linear\_model import LogisticRegression from sklearn.model\_selection import train\_test\_split   df \= pd.read\_csv("loans.csv") X \= pd.get\_dummies(df.drop(columns=\["Approved"\]), drop\_first=True) y \= df\["Approved"\] X\_tr, X\_te, y\_tr, y\_te, g\_tr, g\_te \= train\_test\_split(     X, y, df\["Gender"\], test\_size=0.3, random\_state=42)   model \= LogisticRegression(max\_iter=1000).fit(X\_tr, y\_tr) probs \= model.predict\_proba(X\_te)\[:, 1\] audit \= pd.DataFrame({"gender": g\_te.values, "prob": probs})   \# \--- Baseline: single threshold of 0.5 \--- audit\["pred\_base"\] \= (audit\["prob"\] \>= 0.5).astype(int) print("Before mitigation:") print(audit.groupby("gender")\["pred\_base"\].mean())   \# \--- Post-processing: per-group thresholds to equalise rates \--- \# (Simple illustration: lower threshold for under-approved group) thresholds \= {"female": 0.40, "male": 0.50} audit\["pred\_fair"\] \= audit.apply(     lambda r: int(r\["prob"\] \>= thresholds\[r\["gender"\]\]), axis=1)   print("\\nAfter mitigation:") print(audit.groupby("gender")\["pred\_fair"\].mean())   \# Note: fairness toolkits (AI Fairness 360, Fairlearn) automate this. |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A before-and-after fairness comparison where a mitigation technique (such as per-group threshold adjustment) reduces the disparity in outcome rates, with the fairness-accuracy trade-off noted.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Apply one bias mitigation technique to a biased model and show the reduction in group-wise disparity before and after.

2. Task 2: Explain the difference between pre-processing, in-processing, and post-processing mitigation with an example of each.

3. Task 3: Discuss the trade-off you observed between improving fairness and any change in overall accuracy.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What are the three stages of bias mitigation?**

**Answer:** Pre-processing (adjust the data), in-processing (modify the algorithm), and post-processing (adjust predictions after training).

**Q2. Give one pre-processing mitigation technique.**

**Answer:** Re-balancing an under-represented group through resampling, or reweighting samples, or removing biased features.

**Q3. How can thresholds reduce bias?**

**Answer:** By tuning the decision threshold per group so that group outcome rates become more equal.

**Q4. Name a fairness toolkit.**

**Answer:** AI Fairness 360 or Fairlearn, which package mitigation techniques and standardised fairness metrics.

**Q5. What trade-off does bias mitigation often involve?**

**Answer:** A trade-off between improving fairness and maintaining overall accuracy.

**LAB 09**  
**Week 3  |  Module 2: Ethics & Responsible AI in Practice**

**Explainability with Feature Importance & SHAP Concepts**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Make model predictions explainable using feature importance and understand SHAP-based explanation concepts.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Trustworthy AI must be explainable. Explainability means showing why a model made a prediction in human-understandable terms. For real deployment — especially in regulated domains — being able to justify decisions is often a legal and ethical requirement, not a nicety.

Feature importance is the most accessible explainability tool. Many models (like tree-based models) expose which features most influenced their predictions overall. Ranking features by importance reveals what the model relies on, helping detect both useful signal and problematic reliance on sensitive features.

SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) provides per-prediction explanations grounded in game theory. It assigns each feature a contribution to a specific prediction, showing whether a feature pushed the prediction up or down for that individual case. This local explainability is powerful for understanding single decisions.

Explainability connects directly to fairness and debugging. If a model's top features include a sensitive attribute or its proxy, that signals a fairness risk. If explanations reveal reliance on a spurious feature, that signals a bug. Explanation is thus a tool for both trust and quality control.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | scikit-learn (+ shap concept) |
| **Deliverable** | Ranked feature-importance explanation |
| **Concept** | Global vs local explainability |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Train a tree-based model on a dataset.

2. Extract the model's feature importances.

3. Rank and display the top features.

4. Interpret what the model relies on most.

5. Check whether any sensitive feature ranks highly.

6. Describe how SHAP would explain a single prediction.

7. Summarise the explainability findings.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Explainability: Feature Importance (Colab)   import pandas as pd from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier from sklearn.model\_selection import train\_test\_split   df \= pd.read\_csv("clean\_data.csv") X \= pd.get\_dummies(df.drop(columns=\["Target"\]), drop\_first=True) y \= df\["Target"\] X\_tr, X\_te, y\_tr, y\_te \= train\_test\_split(X, y, test\_size=0.2, random\_state=42)   model \= RandomForestClassifier(n\_estimators=200, random\_state=42).fit(X\_tr, y\_tr)   \# \--- GLOBAL explainability: feature importance \--- importances \= pd.Series(model.feature\_importances\_, index=X.columns) top \= importances.sort\_values(ascending=False).head(10) print("Top 10 features the model relies on:") print(top)   \# \--- Check for sensitive-feature reliance (fairness signal) \--- for feat in top.index:     if "gender" in feat.lower() or "race" in feat.lower():         print(f"WARNING: sensitive feature '{feat}' is influential")   \# \--- LOCAL explainability concept (SHAP) \--- \# import shap \# explainer \= shap.TreeExplainer(model) \# shap\_values \= explainer.shap\_values(X\_te.iloc\[0:1\]) \# \-\> shows each feature's \+ / \- contribution to THIS prediction print("\\nSHAP would explain one prediction feature-by-feature.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A ranked feature-importance explanation for a trained model showing which features drive predictions, a check for influential sensitive features, and a description of how SHAP would explain an individual prediction.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Train a model, extract and rank its feature importances, and interpret which features it relies on most.

2. Task 2: Check whether any sensitive feature is highly influential and explain the fairness implication.

3. Task 3: Describe in your own words how SHAP explains a single prediction differently from global feature importance.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is explainability in AI?**

**Answer:** Showing why a model made a prediction in human-understandable terms, often required for trust and compliance.

**Q2. What does feature importance reveal?**

**Answer:** Which features most influenced the model's predictions overall, exposing what the model relies on.

**Q3. What is SHAP?**

**Answer:** SHapley Additive exPlanations — a method that assigns each feature a contribution to a specific prediction, giving local explanations.

**Q4. What is the difference between global and local explainability?**

**Answer:** Global explains the model's overall behaviour (e.g. feature importance); local explains a single prediction (e.g. SHAP values).

**Q5. How does explainability support fairness?**

**Answer:** If a sensitive feature or proxy is highly influential, explanations flag a fairness risk for investigation.

**LAB 10**  
**Week 4  |  Module 2: Ethics & Responsible AI in Practice**

**Privacy, Data Protection & Anonymization in Practice**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Apply privacy and data protection techniques including anonymization and minimization to a dataset containing personal information.

**▍ Theory / Background**

AI systems frequently process personal data, making privacy protection a core responsibility. Privacy techniques reduce the risk that individuals can be identified or harmed through their data, and many are legally required under data-protection regulations.

Data minimization is the foundational principle: collect and retain only the data actually needed for the task. Removing unnecessary personal fields reduces risk at the source. In practice this means dropping columns that are not required for the analysis or model.

Anonymization removes or obscures information that could identify individuals. Techniques include deleting direct identifiers (names, IDs), masking values, generalising precise data (exact age to age range, full postcode to region), and aggregating. Pseudonymization replaces identifiers with codes, allowing linkage without exposing identity.

A subtle risk is re-identification: combining several non-identifying fields can sometimes single out an individual. True anonymization must consider these quasi-identifiers together. Applying minimization and generalisation thoughtfully — and testing whether records remain uniquely identifiable — is the practical skill.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | pandas |
| **Deliverable** | An anonymized, minimized dataset |
| **Techniques** | Minimization, masking, generalisation |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Load a dataset containing personal information.

2. Identify direct identifiers and unnecessary fields.

3. Drop fields not needed for the task (minimization).

4. Remove or mask direct identifiers.

5. Generalise a precise field (e.g. age to age band).

6. Check whether any records remain uniquely identifiable.

7. Save the anonymized dataset.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Privacy: Minimization & Anonymization (Colab)   import pandas as pd   df \= pd.read\_csv("customers.csv") \# columns: Name, CNIC, Email, Age, Postcode, City, Purchase   \# \--- 1\. Data minimization: drop unneeded personal fields \--- df \= df.drop(columns=\["Email"\])   \# not needed for analysis   \# \--- 2\. Remove direct identifiers \--- df \= df.drop(columns=\["Name", "CNIC"\])   \# \--- 3\. Generalisation: precise \-\> ranges \--- df\["AgeBand"\] \= pd.cut(df\["Age"\], bins=\[0, 25, 40, 60, 100\],                        labels=\["\<=25", "26-40", "41-60", "60+"\]) df \= df.drop(columns=\["Age"\])   \# Generalise postcode to a broader region (first 3 chars) df\["Region"\] \= df\["Postcode"\].astype(str).str\[:3\] df \= df.drop(columns=\["Postcode"\])   \# \--- 4\. Re-identification check (quasi-identifiers) \--- quasi \= \["AgeBand", "City", "Region"\] unique\_rows \= df.groupby(quasi).size() risky \= (unique\_rows \== 1).sum() print(f"Uniquely identifiable combinations: {risky}")   df.head() |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

An anonymized dataset with unnecessary fields removed, direct identifiers deleted, a precise field generalised, and a re-identification check reporting how many records remain uniquely identifiable.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Take a dataset with personal data and anonymize it through minimization, identifier removal, and generalisation, then check for re-identification risk.

2. Task 2: Explain the difference between anonymization and pseudonymization with an example of each.

3. Task 3: Identify a set of quasi-identifiers in a dataset that together could re-identify someone.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is data minimization?**

**Answer:** Collecting and retaining only the data actually needed for the task, reducing privacy risk at the source.

**Q2. What is anonymization?**

**Answer:** Removing or obscuring information that could identify individuals, such as deleting identifiers or generalising precise data.

**Q3. What is pseudonymization?**

**Answer:** Replacing identifiers with codes, allowing data to be linked without directly exposing identity.

**Q4. What is re-identification risk?**

**Answer:** The risk that combining several non-identifying fields (quasi-identifiers) can single out an individual.

**Q5. How does generalisation protect privacy?**

**Answer:** By replacing precise values with broader ranges or regions, making individuals harder to single out.

**LAB 11**  
**Week 4  |  Module 2: Ethics & Responsible AI in Practice**

**Responsible AI Governance & Model Documentation**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Apply responsible AI governance by documenting a model with a model card and assessing it against a responsible-AI checklist.

**▍ Theory / Background**

As models move toward deployment, governance ensures they are used responsibly. Responsible AI governance is the framework of documentation, review, and accountability that surrounds a model throughout its life. Documentation is its cornerstone — undocumented models cannot be governed.

A model card is a standardised document describing a model: its purpose, training data, performance, intended use, limitations, and ethical considerations. Popularised across the industry, model cards make a model's capabilities and risks transparent to users, auditors, and stakeholders.

A responsible-AI checklist operationalises principles into concrete checks: Was the data sourced ethically? Was bias tested? Is the model explainable? Are limitations documented? Is there human oversight? Working through such a checklist before deployment catches issues that pure performance metrics miss.

Governance also defines accountability and monitoring. Someone must own the model's behaviour, and its performance and fairness must be monitored over time, since data drift can degrade both. Documenting these responsibilities and review cadences turns good intentions into durable practice.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Document editor (+ Colab metrics) |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Deliverable** | A completed model card |
| **Activity** | Responsible-AI checklist |
| **Reference** | A model you trained earlier |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Choose a model you trained in a previous lab.

2. Document its purpose and intended use.

3. Record its training data and performance metrics.

4. State its known limitations and risks.

5. Note ethical considerations (bias, privacy).

6. Complete a responsible-AI checklist for it.

7. Compile the model card.

**▍ Model Card & Checklist Template**

| \# MODEL CARD TEMPLATE \# Fill this for a model you trained.   \#\# Model Details \- Name / version : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Type           : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ (e.g. RandomForest classifier) \- Owner          : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Date           : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_   \#\# Intended Use \- Purpose        : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Intended users : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Out-of-scope   : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ (what NOT to use it for)   \#\# Training Data \- Source         : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Size / features: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Known gaps     : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_   \#\# Performance \- Metric(s)      : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ (e.g. accuracy, F1) \- Result         : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- By-group result: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ (fairness)   \#\# Limitations & Risks \- Limitations    : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Bias tested?   : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Privacy notes  : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_   \#\# Responsible-AI Checklist \[ \] Data sourced ethically   \[ \] Bias tested \[ \] Model explainable        \[ \] Limitations documented \[ \] Human oversight defined  \[ \] Monitoring planned |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A completed model card documenting a trained model's purpose, data, performance (including by-group results), limitations, and ethical considerations, plus a completed responsible-AI checklist.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Write a complete model card for a model you trained, including intended use, performance, limitations, and ethical considerations.

2. Task 2: Complete a responsible-AI checklist for the model and flag any item it fails.

3. Task 3: Define who would own the model in production and what would be monitored over time.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is responsible AI governance?**

**Answer:** The framework of documentation, review, and accountability surrounding a model throughout its life to ensure responsible use.

**Q2. What is a model card?**

**Answer:** A standardised document describing a model's purpose, data, performance, intended use, limitations, and ethical considerations.

**Q3. Why document a model's limitations?**

**Answer:** So users understand what the model cannot do and where it may fail, preventing misuse.

**Q4. What does a responsible-AI checklist operationalise?**

**Answer:** It turns principles into concrete checks like ethical data sourcing, bias testing, explainability, and human oversight.

**Q5. Why is ongoing monitoring part of governance?**

**Answer:** Because data drift can degrade performance and fairness over time, so models must be watched after deployment.

**LAB 12**  
**Week 4  |  Module 2: Ethics & Responsible AI in Practice**

**Responsible AI Case Study & Mitigation Plan**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Analyse a real responsible-AI failure case and produce a mitigation plan that addresses its root causes.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Real-world AI failures are powerful teachers. Studying cases where AI caused harm — biased hiring tools, unfair credit systems, flawed facial recognition — reveals how good intentions go wrong and how the principles learned in this module would have prevented the harm.

Effective case analysis follows a structure: what happened, who was affected, what went wrong technically and organisationally, and why. Most failures trace to identifiable root causes — biased data, missing fairness testing, lack of explainability, no human oversight, or poor governance.

A mitigation plan translates analysis into prevention. For each root cause, it specifies a concrete safeguard: better data collection, mandatory bias audits, explainability requirements, human-in-the-loop review, or governance processes. The plan shows how the failure could have been avoided.

This synthesis exercise connects the whole module — bias detection, mitigation, explainability, privacy, and governance — to a real situation. It demonstrates that responsible AI is not abstract: it is a set of concrete practices that, applied together, prevent genuine harm.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Research \+ document editor |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Deliverable** | Case analysis \+ mitigation plan |
| **Activity** | Root-cause analysis |
| **Reference** | A documented real AI failure |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Choose a documented real-world responsible-AI failure.

2. Summarise what happened and who was affected.

3. Identify the technical and organisational root causes.

4. Map each root cause to a module concept (bias, privacy, governance).

5. Propose a concrete safeguard for each root cause.

6. Assemble the mitigation plan.

7. State how the plan would have prevented the harm.

**▍ Case Analysis & Plan Template**

| \# RESPONSIBLE-AI CASE ANALYSIS \+ MITIGATION PLAN   \#\# Case Summary \- What happened : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Who affected  : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ \- Harm caused   : \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_   \#\# Root-Cause Analysis Root cause                | Module concept \------------------------- | \---------------------- Biased training data      | Bias detection No fairness testing       | Bias mitigation Black-box decisions       | Explainability Personal data exposed     | Privacy No oversight / docs       | Governance   \#\# Mitigation Plan (cause \-\> safeguard) Root cause            | Safeguard \--------------------- | \---------------------------- Biased data           | Representative data \+ audit No fairness testing   | Mandatory bias metrics pre-launch Black-box model       | Explainability (feature imp / SHAP) Privacy exposure      | Minimization \+ anonymization No oversight          | Model card \+ human review \+ monitoring   \#\# Conclusion How these safeguards would have prevented the harm: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A structured analysis of a real responsible-AI failure identifying root causes mapped to module concepts, with a mitigation plan pairing each cause to a concrete safeguard and a conclusion on prevention.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Analyse a real responsible-AI failure case and produce a mitigation plan addressing each root cause.

2. Task 2: Map each root cause to the specific module concept (bias, privacy, explainability, or governance) that addresses it.

3. Task 3: Write a one-paragraph 'lesson learned' that a team could adopt as a principle.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. Why study real AI failure cases?**

**Answer:** They reveal how AI causes harm and how responsible-AI principles would have prevented it, making the lessons concrete.

**Q2. What does case analysis examine?**

**Answer:** What happened, who was affected, what went wrong technically and organisationally, and why.

**Q3. What are common root causes of AI failures?**

**Answer:** Biased data, missing fairness testing, lack of explainability, no human oversight, and poor governance.

**Q4. What does a mitigation plan do?**

**Answer:** It pairs each root cause with a concrete safeguard that would prevent the failure from recurring.

**Q5. How does this exercise tie the module together?**

**Answer:** It connects bias, mitigation, explainability, privacy, and governance to a real situation, showing they prevent genuine harm.

**LAB 13**  
**Week 5  |  Module 3: Prompt Engineering — Core to Advanced**

**Advanced Prompting: Structured Output & JSON Mode**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Engineer prompts that return reliable structured output (such as JSON) suitable for use in code and automated pipelines.

**▍ Theory / Background**

In applied AI, prompts often feed into code rather than a human reader. This demands structured output — predictable formats like JSON that programs can parse reliably. Moving from free-text prompting to structured prompting is a key step toward building real AI applications.

Structured output is achieved by explicitly specifying the format in the prompt: defining the exact JSON schema, the field names, and the data types expected. Clear instructions ('Respond only with valid JSON, no extra text') and an example of the desired structure dramatically improve reliability.

Many modern AI APIs offer a dedicated JSON mode or structured-output feature that constrains the model to emit valid JSON. Even without it, careful prompting plus validation in code (attempting to parse the response, retrying on failure) produces robust structured results.

Reliability requires defensive handling. Models can occasionally add stray text or malformed JSON, so production code wraps parsing in error handling, validates the structure against the expected schema, and retries or falls back when output is invalid. Structured prompting plus validation is the professional pattern.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Colab (or an AI playground) |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | JSON schema in prompts |
| **Deliverable** | A prompt returning parseable JSON |
| **Skill** | Parse \+ validate output |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Define a task whose output should be structured (e.g. extracting fields).

2. Specify the exact JSON schema in the prompt.

3. Add an instruction to return only valid JSON.

4. Include one example of the desired JSON.

5. Run the prompt and capture the response.

6. Parse the response as JSON in code, with error handling.

7. Validate that all required fields are present.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Structured Output Prompting \+ Validation (Colab) import json   \# \--- The structured prompt \--- prompt \= '''Extract the following fields from the review and respond with ONLY valid JSON, no extra text.   Schema: {   "sentiment": "positive | neutral | negative",   "rating\_out\_of\_5": integer,   "key\_points": \[string, string\] }   Review: "The delivery was fast and the product is excellent, but the packaging was damaged."'''   \# response \= call\_your\_ai\_api(prompt)   \# returns a string response \= '''{"sentiment": "positive", "rating\_out\_of\_5": 4, "key\_points": \["fast delivery", "damaged packaging"\]}'''   \# \--- Defensive parsing \+ validation \--- def parse\_structured(text):     try:         data \= json.loads(text)     except json.JSONDecodeError:         \# try to recover JSON between first { and last }         start, end \= text.find("{"), text.rfind("}")         data \= json.loads(text\[start:end+1\])     \# validate required fields     required \= \["sentiment", "rating\_out\_of\_5", "key\_points"\]     for field in required:         assert field in data, f"Missing field: {field}"     return data   result \= parse\_structured(response) print(result\["sentiment"\], "-", result\["rating\_out\_of\_5"\], "/5") print("Points:", result\["key\_points"\]) |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A prompt that reliably returns JSON matching a defined schema, with code that parses the response defensively, recovers from minor formatting issues, and validates that all required fields are present.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Engineer a prompt that returns structured JSON for a defined schema, then parse and validate the output in code.

2. Task 2: Add error handling that retries or recovers when the model returns slightly malformed JSON.

3. Task 3: Design a JSON schema for extracting three fields from a product description and test it.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. Why is structured output important in applied AI?**

**Answer:** Because prompts often feed into code, which needs predictable, parseable formats like JSON rather than free text.

**Q2. How do you get structured output from a prompt?**

**Answer:** By specifying the exact format and schema, instructing the model to return only valid JSON, and giving an example.

**Q3. What is JSON mode?**

**Answer:** A feature of some AI APIs that constrains the model to emit valid JSON output.

**Q4. Why wrap JSON parsing in error handling?**

**Answer:** Because models can occasionally add stray text or malformed JSON, so code must parse defensively and validate.

**Q5. What is the professional pattern for structured output?**

**Answer:** Structured prompting combined with parsing, validation against the schema, and retry or fallback on invalid output.

**LAB 14**  
**Week 5  |  Module 3: Prompt Engineering — Core to Advanced**

**Chain-of-Thought & Reasoning Prompts**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Apply chain-of-thought and reasoning techniques to improve model performance on multi-step and logical tasks.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Complex tasks benefit from explicit reasoning. Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting asks the model to work through a problem step by step before giving an answer, mirroring how humans solve hard problems. This often dramatically improves accuracy on maths, logic, and multi-step reasoning.

The core technique is simple: add instructions like 'Let's think step by step' or 'Show your reasoning before the final answer'. This prompts the model to generate intermediate steps, which both improves correctness and makes the reasoning visible for checking.

Variations extend the idea. Few-shot CoT provides worked examples showing the reasoning process, which guides the model on harder tasks. Self-consistency runs the reasoning multiple times and takes the most common answer, improving reliability on tricky problems.

Reasoning prompts also aid debugging and trust. Because the steps are visible, you can spot where the model went wrong and verify the logic. The trade-off is longer, slower responses, so CoT is best reserved for tasks that genuinely require multi-step reasoning rather than simple lookups.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Colab (or an AI playground) |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Step-by-step reasoning |
| **Deliverable** | CoT vs direct prompt comparison |
| **Variation** | Few-shot CoT |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Choose a multi-step reasoning problem (maths or logic).

2. Run a direct prompt asking only for the answer.

3. Run a chain-of-thought prompt asking for step-by-step reasoning.

4. Compare the correctness of both answers.

5. Add a worked example to create a few-shot CoT prompt.

6. Note where the reasoning made the answer verifiable.

7. Record which approach performed best.

**▍ Prompt Comparison (Colab)**

| \# Chain-of-Thought Prompting (Colab)   \# \--- Direct prompt (often less reliable on reasoning) \--- direct \= "If a shop sells 3 pens for 45 rupees, how much do 7 pens cost? Give only the number."   \# \--- Chain-of-thought prompt \--- cot \= '''Solve step by step, then give the final answer.   Question: If a shop sells 3 pens for 45 rupees, how much do 7 pens cost?   Reasoning:'''   \# Expected CoT-style model output: \# Step 1: Price per pen \= 45 / 3 \= 15 rupees \# Step 2: 7 pens \= 7 x 15 \= 105 rupees \# Final answer: 105 rupees   \# \--- Few-shot CoT: show a worked example first \--- few\_shot\_cot \= '''Example: Q: 2 books cost 60 rupees. How much do 5 books cost? A: Per book \= 60/2 \= 30\. 5 books \= 5x30 \= 150\. Answer: 150\.   Now solve: Q: 3 pens cost 45 rupees. How much do 7 pens cost? A:'''   \# Self-consistency idea: run the CoT prompt N times, \# take the most common final answer for reliability. print("Use CoT for multi-step reasoning; direct prompts for simple lookups.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A comparison of a direct prompt versus a chain-of-thought prompt on a multi-step problem, showing improved correctness with visible reasoning, plus a few-shot CoT variation and a note on when to use each.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Compare a direct prompt and a chain-of-thought prompt on a multi-step reasoning problem, and report which performed better.

2. Task 2: Build a few-shot chain-of-thought prompt with one worked example for a logic task.

3. Task 3: Explain the self-consistency technique and when it would be worth the extra cost.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is chain-of-thought prompting?**

**Answer:** Asking the model to work through a problem step by step before answering, which improves accuracy on multi-step tasks.

**Q2. How do you trigger chain-of-thought?**

**Answer:** With instructions like 'Let's think step by step' or 'Show your reasoning before the final answer'.

**Q3. What is few-shot chain-of-thought?**

**Answer:** Providing worked examples that show the reasoning process, guiding the model on harder tasks.

**Q4. What is self-consistency?**

**Answer:** Running the reasoning multiple times and taking the most common answer to improve reliability.

**Q5. When should you avoid chain-of-thought?**

**Answer:** On simple lookups, since it makes responses longer and slower without benefit; it suits genuine multi-step reasoning.

**LAB 15**  
**Week 5  |  Module 3: Prompt Engineering — Core to Advanced**

**Prompt Templates, Variables & Reusable Prompt Functions**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Build reusable prompt templates with variables and wrap them in Python functions for programmatic, repeatable prompting.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Applied AI rarely uses one-off prompts — it uses prompts at scale, applied to many inputs. Prompt templates with variables make this possible: a fixed prompt structure with placeholders that get filled with different data each time, ensuring consistency across thousands of calls.

In Python, templating is natural. An f-string or .format() inserts variables into a prompt template, so the same well-engineered prompt runs on any input. This separates the prompt design (done once) from the data (which varies), a clean and maintainable pattern.

Wrapping prompts in functions takes this further. A function like classify\_sentiment(text) builds the prompt, calls the AI, parses the result, and returns clean output — hiding all the prompt details behind a simple, reusable interface. This is how prompts become building blocks in real software.

Good prompt functions are robust and documented. They validate inputs, handle errors, and return structured results. Building a small library of such functions — for classification, extraction, summarisation — turns prompt engineering into reliable, reusable software components that power larger applications.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Templated, parameterised prompts |
| **Deliverable** | A reusable prompt function |
| **Skill** | Apply one prompt to many inputs |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Design a prompt template with one or more variable placeholders.

2. Write a Python function that fills the template with an input.

3. Have the function call the AI (or a stub) and return the result.

4. Add input validation and error handling.

5. Apply the function to a list of several inputs in a loop.

6. Collect the results into a structured form.

7. Confirm the same prompt works consistently across inputs.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Reusable Prompt Functions (Colab) import json   \# \--- A prompt template with a variable \--- SENTIMENT\_TEMPLATE \= '''Classify the sentiment of this text as positive, neutral, or negative. Respond with ONLY one word.   Text: "{text}"'''   def call\_ai(prompt):     \# Replace with a real API call. Stubbed here for illustration.     return "positive"   def classify\_sentiment(text):     """Reusable prompt function: text \-\> sentiment label."""     if not text or not isinstance(text, str):         raise ValueError("text must be a non-empty string")     prompt \= SENTIMENT\_TEMPLATE.format(text=text)     try:         result \= call\_ai(prompt).strip().lower()     except Exception as e:         return {"text": text, "sentiment": "error", "detail": str(e)}     return {"text": text, "sentiment": result}   \# \--- Apply ONE prompt to MANY inputs \--- reviews \= \[     "Great product, fast delivery\!",     "It was okay, nothing special.",     "Terrible experience, very disappointed.", \] results \= \[classify\_sentiment(r) for r in reviews\] for r in results:     print(f"{r\['sentiment'\]:\<10} \<- {r\['text'\]}") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A reusable prompt function built on a variable template that validates input, calls the AI, handles errors, and returns structured results — demonstrated by applying it across a list of several inputs.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a reusable prompt function from a variable template and apply it to a list of at least three different inputs.

2. Task 2: Add a second prompt function (e.g. extraction or summarisation) to build a small prompt library.

3. Task 3: Add input validation and error handling so the function fails gracefully on bad input.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. Why use prompt templates with variables?**

**Answer:** To apply one well-engineered prompt to many different inputs consistently and at scale.

**Q2. How are templates filled in Python?**

**Answer:** With f-strings or .format(), which insert variables into the placeholder positions of the template.

**Q3. What is the benefit of wrapping a prompt in a function?**

**Answer:** It hides prompt details behind a simple reusable interface that builds the prompt, calls the AI, and returns clean output.

**Q4. What makes a prompt function robust?**

**Answer:** Validating inputs, handling errors, and returning structured, predictable results.

**Q5. How does this turn prompting into software?**

**Answer:** Reusable prompt functions become building blocks that can be combined into larger, reliable AI applications.

**LAB 16**  
**Week 6  |  Module 3: Prompt Engineering — Core to Advanced**

**Role, System & Persona Prompts in API Calls**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Use system messages and role-based prompts within API-style message structures to control model behaviour precisely.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Professional AI applications control model behaviour through structured messages, not just a single prompt. Modern chat APIs use a message format with roles — typically system, user, and assistant — that together shape every response. Mastering this structure is essential for building reliable applications.

The system message sets the model's persona, rules, and context for the entire conversation. It is the most powerful lever: defining the assistant as 'a precise financial analyst who only uses provided data' steers all subsequent responses. The system message is set once and governs the whole exchange.

User and assistant messages form the conversation. User messages are inputs; assistant messages are the model's prior replies, which can be included to maintain context across turns. This message history lets the model remember earlier exchanges, enabling coherent multi-turn interactions.

Combining a strong system message with well-structured user messages gives precise control. The system message enforces consistent persona and constraints; user messages carry the varying task. This separation — persona and rules in system, task in user — is the foundation of building dependable AI features.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Colab (API message structure) |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | system / user / assistant roles |
| **Deliverable** | A role-structured API call |
| **Skill** | Persona control via system message |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Define a persona and rules for the assistant.

2. Write a system message capturing that persona and its constraints.

3. Add a user message carrying the task.

4. Assemble the messages list in the API format.

5. Run the call (or stub) and observe the persona's effect.

6. Add a prior assistant message to test multi-turn context.

7. Note how the system message shaped the output.

**▍ API Message Structure (Colab)**

| \# Role / System / Persona Prompts (API message format)   \# Modern chat APIs take a list of role-tagged messages. messages \= \[     {         "role": "system",         "content": (             "You are a precise financial assistant. You only use "             "facts the user provides. If information is missing, you "             "say so rather than guessing. Keep answers under 60 words."         )     },     {         "role": "user",         "content": "Our revenue was 2.4M and costs were 1.8M. What was profit?"     } \]   \# response \= client.chat.completions.create(model=..., messages=messages) \# The SYSTEM message governs tone, rules, and persona for the whole chat.   \# \--- Multi-turn: include prior assistant reply for context \--- messages.append({"role": "assistant", "content": "Profit was 0.6M."}) messages.append({"role": "user", "content": "What is the profit margin?"}) \# Model now remembers revenue=2.4M and profit=0.6M from context.   print("System sets persona/rules; user carries the task; "       "assistant history preserves context.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A role-structured API-style call with a system message defining a persona and constraints, a user task message, and a multi-turn extension showing context retention — with a note on how the system message shaped behaviour.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a role-structured message list with a strong system persona and a user task, then extend it to a multi-turn exchange.

2. Task 2: Write two different system messages for the same task and describe how each would change the output.

3. Task 3: Explain how the system message differs in effect from putting the same instructions in a user message.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What roles does a chat API message structure use?**

**Answer:** Typically system, user, and assistant, which together shape every response.

**Q2. What does the system message do?**

**Answer:** It sets the model's persona, rules, and context for the entire conversation and is the most powerful behaviour lever.

**Q3. What is the role of assistant messages in the history?**

**Answer:** They represent the model's prior replies, included to maintain context across multi-turn conversations.

**Q4. Why separate persona (system) from task (user)?**

**Answer:** It gives precise control — consistent persona and constraints from the system, with the varying task carried by user messages.

**Q5. How does message history enable multi-turn chat?**

**Answer:** Including prior user and assistant messages lets the model remember earlier exchanges and respond coherently.
