**LAB 34**  
**Week 12  |  Module 6: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Systems**

**RAG Foundations: Why Retrieval, Embeddings & Vectors**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Explain why retrieval-augmented generation is needed and understand the core concepts of embeddings and vector representations.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Language models have two key limitations: a fixed knowledge cutoff and no access to private or specific data. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) overcomes both by retrieving relevant information and supplying it to the model as context, grounding responses in real, current, or private knowledge.

RAG works by combining a retriever and a generator. When a question arrives, the retriever finds the most relevant pieces of information from a knowledge base, and the generator (the LLM) answers using that retrieved context. This lets a model answer accurately about documents it was never trained on.

The magic of retrieval lies in embeddings. An embedding is a numerical vector that represents the meaning of text. Texts with similar meanings have similar vectors, so meaning becomes measurable as distance in vector space. This semantic representation is what makes intelligent retrieval possible.

Vectors enable semantic search: instead of matching keywords, you convert a query into a vector and find the stored text vectors closest to it, retrieving by meaning rather than exact words. Understanding embeddings and vector similarity is the conceptual foundation of every RAG system.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | RAG, embeddings, vector similarity |
| **Deliverable** | An embedding-similarity demo |
| **Skill** | Measure meaning as distance |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Explain why a model needs RAG to answer about private data.

2. Describe the retriever-generator split.

3. Generate embeddings for a few sample sentences.

4. Compute similarity between pairs of sentences.

5. Show that similar-meaning sentences are closer.

6. Explain how a query would retrieve the closest text.

7. Summarise the RAG concept.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# RAG Foundations: Embeddings & Similarity (Colab) import numpy as np   \# An embedding turns text into a vector capturing MEANING. \# (In practice, use an embeddings API/model; illustrative here.) def cosine\_similarity(a, b):     a, b \= np.array(a), np.array(b)     return np.dot(a, b) / (np.linalg.norm(a) \* np.linalg.norm(b))   \# Pretend these are embeddings returned by a model: emb \= {     "How do I reset my password?":   np.array(\[0.90, 0.10, 0.20\]),     "I forgot my login details":     np.array(\[0.86, 0.14, 0.24\]),  \# similar     "What is the weather today?":     np.array(\[0.10, 0.92, 0.30\]),  \# different }   query \= emb\["How do I reset my password?"\] print("Similarity to the query:") for text, vec in emb.items():     print(f"  {cosine\_similarity(query, vec):.3f}  \<- {text}")   \# Highest similarity \= most relevant text to retrieve. \# This is SEMANTIC search: by meaning, not keywords. print("\\nRAG \= retrieve closest text by meaning, then let the LLM answer with it.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A demo computing cosine similarity between sentence embeddings, showing that similar-meaning sentences score higher, with an explanation of how this semantic similarity drives retrieval in RAG.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Compute the similarity between several sentence embeddings and show that similar meanings score higher.

2. Task 2: Explain in your own words why RAG is needed to answer questions about private documents.

3. Task 3: Describe the role of the retriever and the generator in a RAG system.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What problem does RAG solve?**

**Answer:** It overcomes a model's fixed knowledge cutoff and lack of private data by retrieving relevant information as context.

**Q2. How does RAG work?**

**Answer:** A retriever finds the most relevant information from a knowledge base, and the generator answers using that retrieved context.

**Q3. What is an embedding?**

**Answer:** A numerical vector representing the meaning of text, where similar meanings produce similar vectors.

**Q4. What is semantic search?**

**Answer:** Retrieving text by meaning — converting a query to a vector and finding the closest stored vectors — rather than matching keywords.

**Q5. Why are embeddings central to RAG?**

**Answer:** Because they make meaning measurable as vector distance, enabling intelligent retrieval of relevant content.

**LAB 35**  
**Week 12  |  Module 6: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Systems**

**Generating Embeddings & Building a Vector Store**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Generate text embeddings programmatically and store them in a vector store to enable semantic retrieval.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Building a RAG system starts with turning a knowledge base into embeddings and storing them for fast retrieval. This session moves from concept to implementation: generating real embeddings for documents and placing them in a vector store that supports similarity search.

Embeddings are generated by an embedding model accessed via an API or library. Each chunk of text becomes a fixed-length vector. The same model must be used for both stored documents and incoming queries, so they share the same vector space and can be compared meaningfully.

A vector store (or vector database) is a system optimised for storing embeddings and finding the nearest vectors to a query quickly. Options range from simple in-memory solutions (using libraries like FAISS) to managed databases (Chroma, Pinecone, Weaviate). For learning, an in-memory store is ideal.

The stored representation pairs each vector with its source text (and often metadata). When a query arrives, it is embedded and compared against all stored vectors to find the closest matches, returning the associated text. Building this store is the retrieval backbone of any RAG application.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | An embeddings model \+ FAISS/Chroma |
| **Deliverable** | A queryable vector store |
| **Skill** | Embed and store documents |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Prepare a small set of text documents.

2. Generate an embedding for each document.

3. Store each vector paired with its source text.

4. Add the vectors to a vector store (in-memory or library).

5. Embed a sample query with the same model.

6. Retrieve the most similar stored documents.

7. Display the retrieved text.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Generating Embeddings & a Vector Store (Colab) import numpy as np   \# \--- 1\. Knowledge base: text chunks \--- docs \= \[     "To reset your password, click 'Forgot password' on the login page.",     "Our return policy allows refunds within 30 days of purchase.",     "Customer support is available 9am to 6pm, Monday to Friday.", \]   \# \--- 2\. Generate embeddings (use a real embeddings API/model) \--- \# from openai import OpenAI \# def embed(text): \#     return client.embeddings.create(model="text-embedding-3-small", \#                                      input=text).data\[0\].embedding def embed(text):                      \# stub for illustration     np.random.seed(abs(hash(text)) % 10000\)     return np.random.rand(8)   \# \--- 3\. Build an in-memory vector store: (vector, text) pairs \--- store \= \[(embed(d), d) for d in docs\]   \# \--- 4\. Query \-\> embed \-\> retrieve nearest \--- def cosine(a, b):     return np.dot(a, b) / (np.linalg.norm(a) \* np.linalg.norm(b))   def retrieve(query, k=1):     q \= embed(query)     ranked \= sorted(store, key=lambda item: cosine(q, item\[0\]), reverse=True)     return \[text for \_, text in ranked\[:k\]\]   print(retrieve("How can I get my money back?", k=1)) \# A real model would correctly match the return-policy chunk. \# Production: use FAISS, Chroma, Pinecone, or Weaviate for scale. |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A queryable vector store that embeds a set of documents, stores each vector with its source text, embeds an incoming query, and retrieves the most similar documents by vector similarity.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a vector store from a small set of documents and retrieve the most relevant one for a sample query.

2. Task 2: Explain why the same embedding model must be used for documents and queries.

3. Task 3: Name three vector database options and when you would use a managed one over an in-memory store.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. How are embeddings generated?**

**Answer:** By an embedding model accessed via an API or library, which turns each chunk of text into a fixed-length vector.

**Q2. Why must the same model embed documents and queries?**

**Answer:** So they share the same vector space and can be meaningfully compared for similarity.

**Q3. What is a vector store?**

**Answer:** A system optimised for storing embeddings and quickly finding the nearest vectors to a query.

**Q4. Name two vector store options.**

**Answer:** An in-memory library like FAISS, or a managed database like Chroma, Pinecone, or Weaviate.

**Q5. What is stored alongside each vector?**

**Answer:** Its source text and often metadata, so retrieval returns the associated content.

**LAB 36**  
**Week 12  |  Module 6: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Systems**

**Document Chunking & Retrieval Strategies**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Apply document chunking strategies and retrieval techniques (Top-K) to prepare documents for effective RAG.

**▍ Theory / Background**

How documents are split before embedding profoundly affects retrieval quality. Chunking divides long documents into smaller pieces that are embedded and retrieved individually. Good chunking ensures each piece is self-contained and meaningful, so retrieval returns relevant, complete context.

Chunking involves trade-offs. Chunks that are too large dilute relevance and waste context budget; chunks too small lose surrounding meaning. Common strategies split by a fixed token count, by sentences or paragraphs, or with overlap between chunks so context spanning a boundary is not lost.

Retrieval strategy determines what is fetched. Top-K retrieval returns the K most similar chunks to a query — a key parameter. Too few may miss relevant context; too many add noise and cost. Tuning K, and sometimes filtering by metadata, balances completeness against precision.

Effective RAG depends on getting chunking and retrieval right together. Well-sized, overlapping chunks plus an appropriate Top-K give the model focused, sufficient context to answer accurately. These choices are where much of RAG quality engineering happens, before any model is even called.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Chunking \+ Top-K retrieval |
| **Deliverable** | A chunked, retrievable document |
| **Skill** | Tune chunk size and K |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Take a longer document.

2. Split it into chunks by a fixed size with overlap.

3. Embed each chunk and store it.

4. Embed a query and retrieve the Top-K chunks.

5. Inspect whether the retrieved chunks are relevant.

6. Adjust chunk size or K and compare results.

7. Note the trade-offs you observed.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Document Chunking & Top-K Retrieval (Colab)   \# \--- A longer document \--- document \= ("Our company was founded in 2015\. "             "We provide AI consulting services to small businesses. "             "Our return policy allows refunds within 30 days. "             "Support hours are 9am to 6pm on weekdays. "             "We also offer free training workshops every month.")   \# \--- Chunking by words, with overlap \--- def chunk\_text(text, size=12, overlap=4):     words \= text.split()     chunks, i \= \[\], 0     while i \< len(words):         chunks.append(" ".join(words\[i:i \+ size\]))         i \+= size \- overlap          \# overlap preserves boundary context     return chunks   chunks \= chunk\_text(document, size=12, overlap=4) for c in chunks:     print("CHUNK:", c)   \# \--- Top-K retrieval (use real embeddings for similarity) \--- \# 1\. embed each chunk \-\> store \# 2\. embed the query \# 3\. rank chunks by similarity, take top K K \= 2 print(f"\\nRetrieve the top {K} chunks most similar to the query.") print("Chunk too big \-\> dilutes relevance; too small \-\> loses context.") print("K too low \-\> misses context; K too high \-\> adds noise \+ cost.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A document split into overlapping chunks of a chosen size, with Top-K retrieval returning the most similar chunks to a query, and a comparison of how chunk size and K affect relevance.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Chunk a longer document with overlap, retrieve the Top-K chunks for a query, and assess their relevance.

2. Task 2: Compare two chunk sizes on the same query and explain which retrieved better context.

3. Task 3: Explain the trade-offs of setting Top-K too low versus too high.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is chunking?**

**Answer:** Dividing long documents into smaller pieces that are embedded and retrieved individually for better context.

**Q2. What are the trade-offs in chunk size?**

**Answer:** Chunks too large dilute relevance and waste context; chunks too small lose surrounding meaning.

**Q3. Why use overlap between chunks?**

**Answer:** So context spanning a chunk boundary is not lost, improving retrieval of complete information.

**Q4. What is Top-K retrieval?**

**Answer:** Returning the K most similar chunks to a query, where K balances completeness against noise and cost.

**Q5. Why do chunking and retrieval determine RAG quality?**

**Answer:** Because well-sized chunks and an appropriate K give the model focused, sufficient context to answer accurately.

**LAB 37**  
**Week 13  |  Module 6: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Systems**

**Building a Complete RAG Pipeline End to End**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Assemble a complete RAG pipeline that ingests documents, retrieves relevant context for a query, and generates a grounded answer.

**▍ Theory / Background**

This session brings the RAG components together into a working pipeline. A complete RAG system has two phases: indexing (load documents, chunk, embed, store) done once, and querying (embed the question, retrieve relevant chunks, build a prompt with them, generate an answer) done per question.

The querying phase is where retrieval and generation combine. The retrieved chunks are inserted into a prompt as context, with an instruction telling the model to answer using only that context. The model then produces an answer grounded in the retrieved documents rather than its training memory.

Prompt construction is critical. A good RAG prompt clearly delimits the retrieved context, states the question, and instructs the model to answer from the context and to say when the answer is not present. This grounding instruction is what keeps RAG accurate and reduces hallucination.

The end-to-end pipeline ties together every RAG skill: embeddings, vector storage, chunking, retrieval, and grounded generation. The result is a system that can answer questions about a specific document collection accurately — the core capability behind AI assistants over private knowledge.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | Embeddings \+ vector store \+ AI client |
| **Deliverable** | A working RAG pipeline |
| **Skill** | Index then query for grounded answers |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Load and chunk a small document collection.

2. Embed the chunks and store them (indexing phase).

3. Take a user question and embed it.

4. Retrieve the Top-K relevant chunks.

5. Build a prompt inserting the chunks as context.

6. Instruct the model to answer only from the context.

7. Generate and display the grounded answer.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Complete RAG Pipeline (Colab) import numpy as np   \# \====== INDEXING PHASE (run once) \====== docs \= \[     "Refunds are available within 30 days of purchase.",     "Support hours are 9am to 6pm on weekdays.",     "Free training workshops are held every month.", \] def embed(t):                       \# use a real embeddings model     np.random.seed(abs(hash(t)) % 9999); return np.random.rand(8) store \= \[(embed(d), d) for d in docs\]   \# \====== QUERYING PHASE (per question) \====== def cosine(a, b):     return np.dot(a, b) / (np.linalg.norm(a) \* np.linalg.norm(b))   def retrieve(query, k=2):     q \= embed(query)     ranked \= sorted(store, key=lambda x: cosine(q, x\[0\]), reverse=True)     return \[t for \_, t in ranked\[:k\]\]   def rag\_answer(question):     context \= "\\n".join(f"- {c}" for c in retrieve(question))     prompt \= (f"Answer the question using ONLY the context below. "               f"If the answer is not in the context, say "               f"'I don't have that information.'\\n\\n"               f"Context:\\n{context}\\n\\nQuestion: {question}\\nAnswer:")     \# return call\_ai(prompt)     return prompt   \# show the grounded prompt   print(rag\_answer("How long do I have to get a refund?")) |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A working RAG pipeline that indexes a document collection, retrieves relevant chunks for a question, constructs a grounded prompt instructing the model to answer only from the context, and produces a grounded answer.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a complete RAG pipeline that indexes documents and answers a question using retrieved context with a grounding instruction.

2. Task 2: Test your pipeline with a question whose answer is NOT in the documents and confirm it declines to answer.

3. Task 3: Explain the two phases of a RAG system and what happens in each.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What are the two phases of a RAG system?**

**Answer:** Indexing (load, chunk, embed, store) done once, and querying (embed question, retrieve, prompt, generate) done per question.

**Q2. How are retrieval and generation combined?**

**Answer:** Retrieved chunks are inserted into a prompt as context, and the model answers using that context.

**Q3. Why instruct the model to answer only from the context?**

**Answer:** To keep the answer grounded in the retrieved documents and reduce hallucination.

**Q4. What should a RAG prompt include?**

**Answer:** Clearly delimited context, the question, and an instruction to answer from the context or say when the answer is absent.

**Q5. What capability does an end-to-end RAG pipeline provide?**

**Answer:** Accurately answering questions about a specific document collection, the basis of AI assistants over private knowledge.

**LAB 38**  
**Week 13  |  Module 6: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Systems**

**Evaluating & Improving RAG Quality**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Evaluate RAG output quality and apply techniques to improve retrieval relevance and answer faithfulness.

**▍ Theory / Background**

A RAG system that retrieves poorly or answers unfaithfully is worse than useless. Evaluating RAG quality means measuring two things: retrieval quality (are the right chunks retrieved?) and generation quality (is the answer faithful to the retrieved context and actually correct?).

Retrieval evaluation checks whether the relevant information was retrieved for a query. A simple approach is a test set of questions with their known relevant chunks, measuring how often the right chunk appears in the Top-K. Poor retrieval means the answer cannot be correct regardless of the model.

Generation evaluation checks faithfulness and correctness. Faithfulness asks whether the answer is supported by the retrieved context (no hallucination); correctness asks whether it actually answers the question. These can be assessed against reference answers or with criteria-based scoring.

Improvement techniques target each weakness. Poor retrieval is improved by better chunking, tuning K, or improving embeddings. Poor faithfulness is improved by stronger grounding instructions and prompt design. Systematic evaluation reveals which lever to pull, turning RAG tuning into evidence-based engineering.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Retrieval \+ faithfulness evaluation |
| **Deliverable** | A RAG evaluation \+ improvement |
| **Skill** | Diagnose and fix RAG weaknesses |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Build a small test set of questions with known relevant chunks.

2. Run retrieval and measure how often the right chunk is in Top-K.

3. Generate answers and check faithfulness to the context.

4. Identify whether retrieval or generation is the weak point.

5. Apply a targeted improvement (chunking, K, or prompt).

6. Re-evaluate and compare.

7. Record which lever improved quality.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Evaluating & Improving RAG (Colab)   \# \--- Test set: question \-\> the chunk that should be retrieved \--- test\_set \= \[     {"q": "How long for a refund?", "relevant": "refund"},     {"q": "When is support open?",  "relevant": "support"}, \]   def retrieve\_ids(query, k=2):     \# returns labels of retrieved chunks (stub)     table \= {"How long for a refund?": \["refund", "workshop"\],              "When is support open?":  \["support", "refund"\]}     return table.get(query, \[\])\[:k\]   \# \--- 1\. RETRIEVAL evaluation: is the right chunk in Top-K? \--- hits \= 0 for case in test\_set:     got \= retrieve\_ids(case\["q"\], k=2)     found \= case\["relevant"\] in got     hits \+= found     print(f"{'OK ' if found else 'X  '} {case\['q'\]} \-\> {got}") recall \= hits / len(test\_set) print(f"Retrieval recall@2: {recall:.0%}")   \# \--- 2\. FAITHFULNESS check (concept) \--- \# For each answer, verify every claim is supported by the context. \# Unsupported claim \= hallucination \= faithfulness failure.   \# \--- 3\. Improvement levers \--- print("\\nWeak retrieval? \-\> better chunking / tune K / better embeddings") print("Weak faithfulness? \-\> stronger grounding instruction / prompt") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A RAG evaluation measuring retrieval recall against a test set and checking answer faithfulness, identifying the weak point and applying a targeted improvement with before-and-after comparison.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Evaluate a RAG system's retrieval recall and answer faithfulness, then apply one targeted improvement and re-measure.

2. Task 2: Diagnose whether a wrong answer came from poor retrieval or poor generation, and explain how you can tell.

3. Task 3: List the levers to improve retrieval quality versus generation faithfulness.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What two things does RAG evaluation measure?**

**Answer:** Retrieval quality (are the right chunks retrieved?) and generation quality (is the answer faithful and correct?).

**Q2. How is retrieval evaluated?**

**Answer:** With a test set of questions and known relevant chunks, measuring how often the right chunk appears in the Top-K.

**Q3. What is faithfulness in RAG?**

**Answer:** Whether the answer is supported by the retrieved context, with no hallucinated claims.

**Q4. How do you improve poor retrieval?**

**Answer:** Through better chunking, tuning K, or improving the embeddings.

**Q5. How do you improve poor faithfulness?**

**Answer:** With stronger grounding instructions and better prompt design that ties answers to the context.

**LAB 39**  
**Week 13  |  Module 6: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Systems**

**Adding Memory & Conversation to RAG (Chatbot)**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Extend a RAG system with conversation memory to build a multi-turn chatbot that answers follow-up questions over documents.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Single-question RAG is useful, but real assistants hold conversations. Adding memory lets a RAG system handle follow-up questions that depend on earlier turns — 'What about returns?' after 'How long is the warranty?'. This transforms a Q\&A tool into a true document chatbot.

Conversation memory means maintaining the history of the exchange and including it in each new request. The model sees prior questions and answers, so it understands context-dependent follow-ups and pronouns. This is the same message-history pattern from earlier, now combined with retrieval.

A subtlety is that follow-up questions may need their own retrieval. 'What about returns?' should retrieve return-policy chunks, but in isolation it is vague. A common technique is query rewriting: using the conversation history to rephrase the follow-up into a standalone query before retrieval.

Building a RAG chatbot composes everything: retrieval for grounding, conversation history for context, and query handling for follow-ups. The result is a knowledgeable assistant that answers accurately about specific documents while maintaining a natural, coherent conversation — the form most real RAG products take.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | RAG pipeline \+ AI client |
| **Deliverable** | A multi-turn RAG chatbot |
| **Skill** | Maintain memory \+ retrieve per turn |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Start from a working RAG pipeline.

2. Maintain a conversation history list.

3. On each turn, optionally rewrite the follow-up into a standalone query.

4. Retrieve relevant chunks for that query.

5. Build a prompt with history plus retrieved context.

6. Generate the answer and append it to history.

7. Test a multi-turn conversation with a follow-up.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# RAG Chatbot with Memory (Colab)   history \= \[\]   \# list of {"role", "content"} turns   def retrieve(query, k=2):     return \["Returns accepted within 30 days.",             "Warranty covers 1 year from purchase."\]\[:k\]   def rewrite\_followup(question, history):     \# Use history to make a vague follow-up standalone.     \# e.g. "What about returns?" \-\> "What is the returns policy?"     \# prompt \= f"Given the chat history, rewrite the question to be standalone..."     return question   \# stub   def rag\_chat(question):     standalone \= rewrite\_followup(question, history)     context \= "\\n".join(f"- {c}" for c in retrieve(standalone))     convo \= "\\n".join(f"{t\['role'\]}: {t\['content'\]}" for t in history)     prompt \= (f"Conversation so far:\\n{convo}\\n\\n"               f"Context:\\n{context}\\n\\n"               f"Answer the new question using the context. "               f"Question: {question}\\nAnswer:")     \# answer \= call\_ai(prompt)     answer \= "(grounded answer using context \+ history)"     history.append({"role": "user", "content": question})     history.append({"role": "assistant", "content": answer})     return answer   print(rag\_chat("How long is the warranty?")) print(rag\_chat("What about returns?"))   \# follow-up uses memory |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A multi-turn RAG chatbot that maintains conversation history, optionally rewrites follow-up questions into standalone queries, retrieves relevant context per turn, and answers coherently across turns including a context-dependent follow-up.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a multi-turn RAG chatbot with memory that correctly answers a context-dependent follow-up question.

2. Task 2: Demonstrate why a follow-up like 'what about returns?' needs query rewriting before retrieval.

3. Task 3: Explain how conversation memory is maintained and passed to the model each turn.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. Why add memory to a RAG system?**

**Answer:** So it can handle follow-up questions that depend on earlier turns, turning Q\&A into a true conversation.

**Q2. How is conversation memory maintained?**

**Answer:** By keeping the history of the exchange and including prior questions and answers in each new request.

**Q3. Why do follow-up questions need their own retrieval?**

**Answer:** Because they may reference earlier context, so the right chunks must be retrieved for the actual topic.

**Q4. What is query rewriting?**

**Answer:** Using the conversation history to rephrase a vague follow-up into a standalone query before retrieval.

**Q5. What does a RAG chatbot compose?**

**Answer:** Retrieval for grounding, conversation history for context, and query handling for coherent multi-turn answers.

**RAG over Real Documents: PDFs & Knowledge Bases**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Build a RAG system over real documents such as PDFs, handling extraction, chunking, and indexing of actual content.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Real-world RAG operates over actual documents — PDFs, manuals, policies, knowledge bases — not toy text. This session applies the RAG pipeline to genuine files, adding the practical step of extracting clean text from documents before chunking and indexing.

Document extraction is the first hurdle. PDFs and other formats must be parsed into plain text using libraries (such as PyPDF or similar). Real documents bring messiness: headers, footers, tables, and formatting artifacts that should be cleaned so the extracted text embeds meaningfully.

Once extracted and cleaned, the familiar pipeline applies: chunk the text sensibly, embed the chunks, and index them in a vector store. Real documents are larger and more varied than samples, making thoughtful chunking and metadata (such as source page or section) more important for useful retrieval.

The result is a RAG assistant grounded in a real knowledge base — capable of answering questions about a company's actual policies, a product manual, or a research collection. This is the form RAG most often takes in practice, and building it over real files is the key applied skill.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | PDF parser \+ RAG pipeline |
| **Deliverable** | RAG over a real PDF/knowledge base |
| **Skill** | Extract, clean, index real documents |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Obtain a real document (e.g. a PDF policy or manual).

2. Extract its text with a parsing library.

3. Clean the extracted text of artifacts.

4. Chunk the cleaned text with metadata (e.g. page).

5. Embed and index the chunks.

6. Ask questions and retrieve grounded answers.

7. Verify the answers against the source document.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# RAG over a Real PDF (Colab) \# \!pip install pypdf \-q   \# \--- 1\. Extract text from a PDF \--- \# from pypdf import PdfReader \# reader \= PdfReader("policy.pdf") \# pages \= \[(i \+ 1, page.extract\_text() or "") for i, page in enumerate(reader.pages)\] pages \= \[(1, "Refund policy: refunds within 30 days. "),          (2, "Warranty: 1 year parts and labour. ")\]   \# stub   \# \--- 2\. Clean \+ chunk WITH metadata (page number) \--- def clean(t):     return " ".join(t.split())     \# collapse whitespace/artifacts   chunks \= \[\] for page\_no, text in pages:     for piece in clean(text).split(". "):         if piece.strip():             chunks.append({"text": piece.strip(), "page": page\_no})   \# \--- 3\. Embed \+ index each chunk (use real embeddings) \--- \# store \= \[(embed(c\["text"\]), c) for c in chunks\]   \# \--- 4\. Query \-\> retrieve \-\> grounded answer WITH source \--- \# retrieved \= retrieve("how long is the warranty?") \# \-\> include the page number in the answer for traceability for c in chunks:     print(f"\[p{c\['page'\]}\] {c\['text'\]}") print("\\nReal RAG cites the source page so answers are traceable.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A RAG system built over a real document that extracts and cleans the text, chunks it with source metadata such as page numbers, indexes it, and answers questions with traceable, grounded responses.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a RAG system over a real PDF that extracts, cleans, chunks (with page metadata), and answers questions about it.

2. Task 2: Verify two answers against the source document and confirm the cited pages are correct.

3. Task 3: Describe the extra challenges real documents pose compared with toy text and how you handled them.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What does real-world RAG operate over?**

**Answer:** Actual documents like PDFs, manuals, policies, and knowledge bases, not toy text.

**Q2. What is the first practical step for PDF RAG?**

**Answer:** Extracting clean plain text from the document using a parsing library, removing artifacts.

**Q3. Why is metadata like page number useful?**

**Answer:** It makes answers traceable to their source, improving trust and verification.

**Q4. Why is thoughtful chunking more important for real documents?**

**Answer:** Because real documents are larger and more varied, so chunking and metadata strongly affect retrieval usefulness.

**Q5. What does a real-document RAG assistant enable?**

**Answer:** Accurately answering questions about a real knowledge base such as company policies or a product manual.

**LAB 41**  
**Week 14  |  Module 6: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Systems**

**Optimizing RAG: Reranking, Hybrid Search & Cost**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Apply advanced RAG optimizations — reranking, hybrid search, and cost control — to improve quality and efficiency.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Basic RAG can be improved significantly with advanced techniques. As RAG systems move toward production, optimizing retrieval quality and managing cost become priorities. Several established techniques push RAG from working to genuinely reliable and efficient.

Reranking improves retrieval precision. The initial vector retrieval returns candidate chunks by similarity, but a reranking model then re-scores them for true relevance to the query, surfacing the best ones to the top. This two-stage approach (retrieve broadly, then rerank precisely) markedly improves the context quality.

Hybrid search combines semantic and keyword retrieval. Pure vector search can miss exact terms (names, codes, specific phrases), while keyword search misses meaning. Hybrid search runs both and merges results, capturing both semantic relevance and exact matches — more robust than either alone.

Cost control matters at scale. Retrieving fewer but better chunks (via reranking) reduces tokens sent to the model; caching frequent queries avoids repeated work; and choosing efficient models balances quality and spend. Optimizing RAG means improving answer quality while keeping latency and cost sustainable.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Reranking, hybrid search, cost |
| **Deliverable** | An optimized RAG retrieval step |
| **Skill** | Improve precision and efficiency |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Start from a working RAG retrieval step.

2. Retrieve a broad set of candidate chunks.

3. Apply a reranking step to reorder by true relevance.

4. Add a keyword match alongside semantic search (hybrid).

5. Merge the results sensibly.

6. Reduce the chunks sent to the model to control cost.

7. Compare quality and token usage before and after.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Optimizing RAG: Rerank \+ Hybrid \+ Cost (Colab)   query \= "What is the warranty period for the X100 model?"   \# \--- 1\. Broad semantic retrieval (top 10 candidates) \--- \# candidates \= vector\_retrieve(query, k=10) candidates \= \["Warranty is 1 year for all models.",               "The X100 model warranty is 2 years.",               "Refunds within 30 days.", "..."\]   \# \--- 2\. RERANK: re-score candidates for true relevance \--- \# reranked \= rerank\_model(query, candidates)  \# best first \# \-\> surfaces "X100 ... 2 years" above the generic line print("Rerank: retrieve broadly, then re-score precisely \-\> better top chunks.")   \# \--- 3\. HYBRID search: also do keyword match for exact terms \--- def keyword\_hits(query, candidates):     terms \= \[w.lower() for w in query.split() if len(w) \> 3\]     return \[c for c in candidates if any(t in c.lower() for t in terms)\] print("Keyword hits for 'X100':", keyword\_hits(query, candidates)) \# Merge semantic \+ keyword results to catch exact codes/names.   \# \--- 4\. COST control: send only top 2-3 reranked chunks \--- \# Fewer, better chunks \-\> fewer tokens \-\> lower cost \+ latency. \# Cache frequent queries to avoid repeated retrieval \+ generation. print("Send fewer, better chunks; cache frequent queries to cut cost.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

An optimized RAG retrieval step that retrieves broadly then reranks for precision, adds keyword (hybrid) matching for exact terms, and reduces chunks sent to the model for cost control, with quality and token usage compared.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Improve a RAG retrieval step with reranking and hybrid (semantic \+ keyword) search, and compare quality before and after.

2. Task 2: Show a query where pure semantic search misses an exact term that hybrid search catches.

3. Task 3: List three ways to reduce RAG cost without harming answer quality.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What does reranking do?**

**Answer:** It re-scores the initially retrieved candidate chunks for true relevance, surfacing the best ones to the top.

**Q2. What is hybrid search?**

**Answer:** Combining semantic (vector) and keyword retrieval and merging results to capture both meaning and exact matches.

**Q3. Why does pure vector search sometimes fail?**

**Answer:** It can miss exact terms like names, codes, or specific phrases that keyword search would catch.

**Q4. How does reranking help with cost?**

**Answer:** By enabling fewer but better chunks to be sent to the model, reducing tokens and cost.

**Q5. Name a cost-control technique for RAG.**

**Answer:** Sending fewer high-quality chunks, caching frequent queries, or choosing efficient models.

**LAB 42**  
**Week 14  |  Module 6: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Systems**

**Module 6 Capstone: A Domain-Specific RAG Assistant**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Integrate all RAG skills into a complete domain-specific assistant that answers questions accurately over a chosen knowledge base.

**▍ Theory / Background**

This capstone unites every RAG skill into a complete, domain-specific assistant. It demonstrates the ability to build a knowledgeable AI over a real document collection — the single most common and valuable applied-AI pattern in industry today.

A complete RAG assistant has the full architecture: document ingestion and cleaning, sensible chunking with metadata, embedding and vector storage, optimized retrieval (reranking and hybrid where useful), grounded generation, conversation memory, and evaluation. Each element reflects a session from the module.

Building it well means choosing a focused domain (a product manual, a policy set, a subject's notes), ingesting real content, and tuning the pipeline for accurate, traceable answers. The assistant should answer correctly, cite sources, decline when information is absent, and hold a coherent conversation.

This artifact is the centrepiece of Level 2's RAG module and a portfolio-worthy demonstration of applied AI. It proves you can build a grounded, reliable assistant over private knowledge — directly preparing you for the autonomous-agent and deployment work in the final modules.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Library** | Full RAG stack \+ AI client |
| **Deliverable** | A domain-specific RAG assistant |
| **Skill** | Integrate the full RAG pipeline |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Choose a focused domain and gather its documents.

2. Ingest, clean, and chunk the documents with metadata.

3. Embed and index them in a vector store.

4. Add optimized retrieval (rerank/hybrid where useful).

5. Build grounded generation with source citation.

6. Add conversation memory for follow-ups.

7. Evaluate, then present the assistant.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# Module 6 Capstone: Domain-Specific RAG Assistant (Colab)   class RAGAssistant:     def \_\_init\_\_(self):         self.store \= \[\]        \# (embedding, {text, source})         self.history \= \[\]       def ingest(self, docs):    \# docs: list of {text, source}         for d in docs:             for chunk in self.\_chunk(d\["text"\]):                 \# self.store.append((embed(chunk), {"text": chunk,                 \#                                    "source": d\["source"\]}))                 self.store.append((None, {"text": chunk, "source": d\["source"\]}))       def \_chunk(self, text, size=20, overlap=5):         w \= text.split(); out, i \= \[\], 0         while i \< len(w):             out.append(" ".join(w\[i:i+size\])); i \+= size \- overlap         return out       def ask(self, question):         \# 1\. retrieve (semantic \+ rerank \+ hybrid) \-\> top chunks         \# 2\. build grounded prompt with sources \+ history         \# 3\. generate, cite sources, decline if not found         context \= "\\n".join(f"- {c\[1\]\['text'\]} \[src: {c\[1\]\['source'\]}\]"                             for c in self.store\[:2\])         prompt \= (f"Answer ONLY from context; cite the source; if absent "                   f"say you don't know.\\nContext:\\n{context}\\n\\n"                   f"Q: {question}\\nA:")         answer \= "(grounded answer with citation)"         self.history.append((question, answer))         return answer   bot \= RAGAssistant() bot.ingest(\[{"text": "Warranty is 2 years for the X100.", "source": "manual\_p4"}\]) print(bot.ask("How long is the X100 warranty?")) |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A complete domain-specific RAG assistant that ingests and chunks real documents with sources, indexes them, retrieves and generates grounded answers with citations, supports follow-up questions via memory, and has been evaluated — presented end to end.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a complete domain-specific RAG assistant over a chosen knowledge base that answers accurately, cites sources, and handles follow-ups.

2. Task 2: Evaluate your assistant on at least three questions, including one whose answer is absent from the documents.

3. Task 3: Document your assistant's architecture and the optimizations you applied.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What does a complete RAG assistant integrate?**

**Answer:** Document ingestion, chunking with metadata, embedding and storage, optimized retrieval, grounded generation, memory, and evaluation.

**Q2. Why choose a focused domain?**

**Answer:** So the knowledge base is coherent and the assistant can be tuned for accurate, traceable answers within that domain.

**Q3. What should the assistant do when information is absent?**

**Answer:** Decline to answer rather than hallucinate, stating it does not have that information.

**Q4. Why is this the most common applied-AI pattern?**

**Answer:** Because grounding an AI assistant over private knowledge is highly valuable and widely needed across industries.

**Q5. How does this capstone prepare for later modules?**

**Answer:** It proves the ability to build grounded, reliable AI systems, directly preparing for autonomous agents and deployment.

**LAB 43**  
**Week 15  |  Module 7: No-Code AI Application Development**

**No-Code AI App Builders: Landscape & First App**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Survey no-code AI app-building platforms and create a first simple AI-powered application without writing backend code.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Not every AI application needs to be coded from scratch. No-code AI app builders let you create functional, AI-powered apps through visual interfaces, dramatically lowering the barrier to shipping real tools. This module shows how to turn AI capabilities into usable applications quickly.

The landscape includes several categories: app builders like Bubble and Glide (visual app development with AI integrations), AI-specific builders, and rapid-prototyping tools like Streamlit and Gradio (which use minimal Python to create web interfaces). Each suits different needs and skill levels.

These platforms share a model: you design the interface visually or with simple code, connect data sources, and wire in AI through built-in features or API calls. The AI logic learned earlier — prompts, RAG, APIs — plugs into a front-end that users can actually interact with.

A first no-code AI app typically takes user input, sends it to an AI model, and displays the result — a summariser, a classifier, a simple chatbot. Building one demonstrates the whole loop: interface, AI call, and output, proving that a working AI product can be created in hours, not weeks.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Streamlit / Gradio / Bubble / Glide |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | No-code/low-code AI apps |
| **Deliverable** | A first working AI app |
| **Skill** | Wire input \-\> AI \-\> output |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Survey the no-code AI app-builder options.

2. Choose a platform suited to a simple AI app.

3. Design an interface with an input and an output area.

4. Wire the input to an AI model call.

5. Display the AI's response in the output area.

6. Test the app with sample inputs.

7. Note how the interface, AI, and output connect.

**▍ Python Code (Gradio)**

| \# First No-Code/Low-Code AI App with Gradio (Colab) \# \!pip install gradio \-q import gradio as gr   def ai\_summarise(text):     if not text.strip():         return "Please enter some text."     \# prompt \= f"Summarise in one sentence: {text}"     \# return call\_ai(prompt)     return "(AI summary of your text appears here)"   \# \--- Build a UI: input box \-\> AI \-\> output, no backend code \--- app \= gr.Interface(     fn=ai\_summarise,     inputs=gr.Textbox(lines=5, label="Paste text to summarise"),     outputs=gr.Textbox(label="AI Summary"),     title="AI Text Summariser",     description="Enter text and get a one-sentence AI summary.", )   \# app.launch()    \# creates a shareable web app instantly   \# Platform landscape: \#   Streamlit/Gradio : fast Python-based AI web apps \#   Bubble/Glide     : visual app builders with AI integrations print("Input \-\> AI model \-\> output, wrapped in a real interface.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A first working AI-powered app with an input box, an AI processing step, and an output display — built with minimal code on a no-code/low-code platform and tested with sample inputs.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a first AI-powered app (e.g. a summariser or classifier) with an input, an AI call, and an output display.

2. Task 2: Compare two no-code AI platforms and state which suits a non-programmer versus a Python user.

3. Task 3: Add a second input or option to your app and show how it changes the AI behaviour.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What do no-code AI app builders enable?**

**Answer:** Creating functional AI-powered apps through visual or minimal-code interfaces, lowering the barrier to shipping tools.

**Q2. Name two categories of these platforms.**

**Answer:** Visual app builders like Bubble and Glide, and rapid-prototyping tools like Streamlit and Gradio.

**Q3. What model do these platforms share?**

**Answer:** Designing an interface, connecting data, and wiring in AI through built-in features or API calls.

**Q4. What does a first AI app typically do?**

**Answer:** Take user input, send it to an AI model, and display the result, such as a summariser or classifier.

**Q5. How does earlier AI logic fit in?**

**Answer:** Prompts, RAG, and API calls plug into a front-end interface that users can actually interact with.

**LAB 44**  
**Week 15  |  Module 7: No-Code AI Application Development**

**Building an AI Chat Interface with State**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Build an interactive AI chat application that maintains conversation state across turns in a no-code/low-code platform.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Chat is the most common AI app interface. Building a chat application requires more than a single input-output: it must maintain state — the running conversation — so the assistant remembers the exchange. This combines interface design with the message-history pattern from earlier.

Conversation state is the list of messages exchanged so far. Each new user message is added to the state, the full history is sent to the model, and the response is appended. Maintaining this state correctly is what makes the chat coherent rather than a series of disconnected replies.

In low-code tools like Streamlit or Gradio, state is managed with built-in session mechanisms that persist data across interactions. The chat UI displays the message history and provides an input box; behind it, the state holds the conversation and the AI call uses it for context.

A good chat app handles the full loop smoothly: render the history, accept input, update state, call the AI with context, display the reply, and persist for the next turn. Getting this right produces a real conversational assistant — the foundation for chatbots, support tools, and AI companions.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Streamlit / Gradio |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Conversation state management |
| **Deliverable** | A stateful AI chat app |
| **Skill** | Persist and use chat history |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Set up a chat interface with a message display and input.

2. Initialise a conversation-state list.

3. On each user message, append it to the state.

4. Send the full history to the AI model.

5. Append the AI reply to the state.

6. Render the updated conversation.

7. Test a multi-turn conversation for coherence.

**▍ Python Code (Gradio)**

| \# Stateful AI Chat App with Gradio (Colab) \# \!pip install gradio \-q import gradio as gr   def respond(message, history):     \# 'history' is the running conversation state (list of pairs)     context \= "\\n".join(f"User: {u}\\nAI: {a}" for u, a in history)     \# prompt \= f"{context}\\nUser: {message}\\nAI:"     \# reply \= call\_ai(prompt)     reply \= f"(reply using {len(history)} prior turns of context)"     return reply   \# gr.ChatInterface manages conversation STATE automatically chat \= gr.ChatInterface(     fn=respond,     title="AI Chat Assistant",     description="A multi-turn chat that remembers the conversation.", ) \# chat.launch()   \# Manual state pattern (Streamlit): \# if "messages" not in st.session\_state: \#     st.session\_state.messages \= \[\] \# st.session\_state.messages.append({"role": "user", "content": msg}) \# \-\> send full st.session\_state.messages to the model each turn print("Chat \= maintain message history (state) \+ send it as context each turn.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A stateful AI chat application that maintains conversation history across turns, sends the full context to the model for each reply, and renders a coherent multi-turn conversation.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a stateful AI chat app that maintains conversation history and answers a context-dependent follow-up correctly.

2. Task 2: Explain how conversation state is stored and why it must be sent to the model each turn.

3. Task 3: Add a 'clear conversation' button that resets the state and test it.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. Why does a chat app need state?**

**Answer:** To maintain the running conversation so the assistant remembers the exchange and stays coherent across turns.

**Q2. What is conversation state?**

**Answer:** The list of messages exchanged so far, sent to the model as context for each new reply.

**Q3. How is state managed in low-code tools?**

**Answer:** With built-in session mechanisms that persist data across interactions, such as session state.

**Q4. What is the full chat loop?**

**Answer:** Render history, accept input, update state, call the AI with context, display the reply, and persist for the next turn.

**Q5. What does getting chat state right produce?**

**Answer:** A real conversational assistant — the foundation for chatbots, support tools, and AI companions.

**LAB 45**  
**Week 15  |  Module 7: No-Code AI Application Development**

**Integrating RAG into a No-Code AI Application**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Integrate a RAG backend into a no-code/low-code application so users can ask questions over a document set through a friendly interface.

**▍ Theory / Background**

The most valuable AI apps combine a friendly interface with a powerful backend. Integrating RAG into a no-code app gives users a simple way to ask questions over documents — wrapping the RAG pipeline built earlier in an accessible front-end. This is how document assistants reach real users.

The architecture separates concerns: the front-end (built in a no-code/low-code tool) handles user input and display, while the RAG backend (the indexing and retrieval pipeline) does the heavy lifting. The interface calls the RAG logic, which retrieves context and generates a grounded answer.

Integration means connecting the two: the app passes the user's question to the RAG function, receives the grounded answer (ideally with sources), and displays it. Document upload can even be part of the interface, letting users build their own knowledge base through the app.

The result is a complete, usable RAG product — a document Q\&A assistant that anyone can use without seeing the code. This pattern, a clean interface over a RAG backend, is one of the most common and commercially valuable AI applications, and building it demonstrates full-stack applied-AI capability.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Streamlit / Gradio \+ RAG backend |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Interface over a RAG backend |
| **Deliverable** | A no-code RAG document app |
| **Skill** | Wrap RAG in a usable UI |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Reuse a working RAG pipeline as the backend.

2. Build an interface with a question input and answer display.

3. Optionally add document upload to build the knowledge base.

4. Wire the question input to the RAG function.

5. Display the grounded answer with its sources.

6. Test the app with several document questions.

7. Confirm the answers are grounded and traceable.

**▍ Python Code (Gradio \+ RAG)**

| \# No-Code RAG App: interface over a RAG backend (Gradio) \# \!pip install gradio \-q import gradio as gr   \# \--- RAG backend (from Module 6\) \--- def rag\_answer(question, knowledge):     \# 1\. retrieve relevant chunks from 'knowledge'     \# 2\. build grounded prompt, generate answer \+ cite source     return f"(grounded answer to '{question}' with source citation)"   KNOWLEDGE \= \["Warranty is 2 years.", "Refunds within 30 days."\]   def ask(question, file):     kb \= KNOWLEDGE     if file is not None:         \# extract \+ chunk \+ index the uploaded document into kb         pass     return rag\_answer(question, kb)   \# \--- Friendly interface over the RAG backend \--- app \= gr.Interface(     fn=ask,     inputs=\[gr.Textbox(label="Ask about the documents"),             gr.File(label="Upload a document (optional)")\],     outputs=gr.Textbox(label="Grounded Answer"),     title="Document Q\&A Assistant (RAG)", ) \# app.launch() print("A clean UI \+ a RAG backend \= a usable document assistant.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A no-code/low-code RAG application where users ask questions through an interface, the RAG backend retrieves and generates grounded answers with sources, and the answers display in a friendly UI, optionally with document upload.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Integrate a RAG backend into a no-code app so users can ask document questions and receive grounded, sourced answers.

2. Task 2: Add document upload to your app so users can build their own knowledge base.

3. Task 3: Explain how separating the front-end from the RAG backend keeps the app maintainable.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What makes the most valuable AI apps?**

**Answer:** Combining a friendly interface with a powerful backend, such as a clean UI over a RAG pipeline.

**Q2. How does the architecture separate concerns?**

**Answer:** The front-end handles input and display, while the RAG backend handles indexing, retrieval, and generation.

**Q3. What does integration involve?**

**Answer:** Passing the user's question to the RAG function and displaying the grounded answer, ideally with sources.

**Q4. Why include document upload in the interface?**

**Answer:** So users can build their own knowledge base through the app, making it self-service.

**Q5. Why is this pattern commercially valuable?**

**Answer:** A clean interface over a RAG backend is one of the most common and useful AI products across industries.

**LAB 46**  
**Week 16  |  Module 7: No-Code AI Application Development**

**Connecting Databases & External Data to No-Code Apps**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Connect a database or external data source to a no-code AI app so it can read and write persistent data.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Real applications need to store and retrieve data persistently. Connecting a database to a no-code AI app lets it remember information between sessions — saving conversations, storing user data, logging results. This turns a stateless tool into a real application with memory.

Data options range from simple to robust: spreadsheets (Google Sheets as a lightweight database), cloud databases (Firebase, Supabase), and traditional databases. No-code platforms provide connectors to read from and write to these sources, often without writing queries.

The two core operations are reading (fetching stored data to display or use) and writing (saving new data from the app). For an AI app, this might mean logging every question and answer, storing uploaded documents, or saving user preferences. Persistent data enables history, analytics, and personalisation.

Connecting data also enables AI over your own records: the app can fetch data from the database, pass it to the AI for analysis, and store the result. This closes the loop between data storage and AI processing, the foundation of data-driven AI applications that learn from accumulated information.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | No-code app \+ Sheets/Supabase/Firebase |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Persistent data (read/write) |
| **Deliverable** | An AI app with a connected database |
| **Skill** | Store and retrieve app data |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Choose a data store (e.g. Google Sheets or Supabase).

2. Connect it to your no-code AI app.

3. Write data from the app (e.g. log a Q\&A).

4. Read data back from the store to display.

5. Pass stored data to the AI for processing.

6. Save the AI result back to the store.

7. Verify data persists across sessions.

**▍ Python Code (DB \+ AI)**

| \# Connecting a Database to an AI App (conceptual \+ Python)   \# \--- Lightweight option: Google Sheets as a database \--- \# import gspread \# sheet \= gspread.service\_account().open("AppData").sheet1 \# sheet.append\_row(\[question, answer, timestamp\])   \# WRITE \# rows \= sheet.get\_all\_records()                     \# READ   \# \--- Cloud DB option: Supabase \--- \# from supabase import create\_client \# db \= create\_client(URL, KEY) \# db.table("logs").insert({"q": q, "a": a}).execute()   \# WRITE \# data \= db.table("logs").select("\*").execute().data    \# READ   \# \--- The data-driven AI loop \--- def log\_interaction(question, answer):     \# WRITE: persist every Q\&A so the app has memory     print(f"Saved: {question} \-\> {answer}")   def analyse\_history():     \# READ stored Q\&A \-\> send to AI \-\> store insight     \# history \= read\_all()     \# insight \= call\_ai(f"Summarise common questions: {history}")     print("READ data \-\> AI analysis \-\> WRITE insight")   log\_interaction("How long is the warranty?", "2 years") analyse\_history() print("Persistent data turns a stateless tool into a real app.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A no-code AI app connected to a database that writes data (such as logged Q\&A) and reads it back, passes stored data to the AI for processing, saves the result, and persists data across sessions.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Connect a database to an AI app that logs every question and answer and can read the history back.

2. Task 2: Have the app fetch stored data, pass it to the AI for analysis, and save the insight.

3. Task 3: Compare a spreadsheet, Firebase, and Supabase as data backends and when to use each.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. Why connect a database to an AI app?**

**Answer:** To store and retrieve data persistently, giving the app memory between sessions for history, analytics, and personalisation.

**Q2. Name three data backend options.**

**Answer:** Spreadsheets like Google Sheets, cloud databases like Firebase or Supabase, and traditional databases.

**Q3. What are the two core data operations?**

**Answer:** Reading (fetching stored data) and writing (saving new data from the app).

**Q4. What does persistent data enable for an AI app?**

**Answer:** Logging interactions, storing documents, saving preferences, and enabling history and analytics.

**Q5. What is the data-driven AI loop?**

**Answer:** Fetching data from the database, passing it to the AI for analysis, and storing the result.

**LAB 47**  
**Week 16  |  Module 7: No-Code AI Application Development**

**Multi-Feature AI Application Design & Assembly**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Design and assemble a multi-feature AI application that combines several AI capabilities into one coherent product.

**▍ Theory / Background**

Real products do more than one thing. A multi-feature AI application combines several capabilities — for example summarisation, Q\&A, and generation — into a single coherent tool. Designing and assembling such an app demonstrates the ability to build something genuinely useful, not just a demo.

Good multi-feature design starts with the user's needs and organises features logically. An app might have tabs or sections for different functions, a shared data layer, and a consistent interface. Each feature reuses the AI building blocks already mastered — prompt functions, RAG, API calls.

Assembly is an exercise in composition and coherence. The features must work together smoothly, share data where appropriate, and present a unified experience. Clean structure — separating the interface, the AI logic, and the data — keeps a multi-feature app maintainable as it grows.

The result is an application with real depth: multiple useful functions in one place, backed by AI and data. This represents a step from single-purpose tools to genuine products, and the design and assembly skills involved are exactly those needed to build AI applications people will actually use.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Streamlit / Gradio / no-code builder |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Multi-feature app architecture |
| **Deliverable** | A multi-feature AI app |
| **Skill** | Compose features coherently |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Define the user need and the features that serve it.

2. Sketch the app structure (sections or tabs).

3. Reuse AI building blocks for each feature.

4. Build a shared data layer where needed.

5. Assemble the features into one interface.

6. Ensure the features work together coherently.

7. Test the full app across all features.

**▍ Python Code (Streamlit)**

| \# Multi-Feature AI App Architecture (Streamlit-style) \# \!pip install streamlit \-q   \# \--- Reuse AI building blocks (from earlier modules) \--- def summarise(text):   return "(AI summary)" def rag\_qa(question):  return "(grounded answer)" def generate(brief):   return "(generated content)"   \# \--- Multi-feature app with tabs/sections \+ shared data \--- \# import streamlit as st \# st.title("AI Workspace") \# tab1, tab2, tab3 \= st.tabs(\["Summarise", "Ask Docs", "Generate"\]) \# \# with tab1:                     \# Feature 1: summarisation \#     text \= st.text\_area("Text") \#     if st.button("Summarise"): st.write(summarise(text)) \# \# with tab2:                     \# Feature 2: RAG Q\&A \#     q \= st.text\_input("Question") \#     if st.button("Ask"): st.write(rag\_qa(q)) \# \# with tab3:                     \# Feature 3: generation \#     brief \= st.text\_input("Brief") \#     if st.button("Generate"): st.write(generate(brief))   \# CLEAN STRUCTURE: \#   interface layer  (tabs/widgets) \#   AI logic layer   (summarise / rag\_qa / generate) \#   data layer       (shared store) print("Compose multiple AI features into one coherent product.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A multi-feature AI application combining several AI capabilities (such as summarisation, Q\&A, and generation) into one coherent interface with a shared data layer, tested across all features.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Design and assemble a multi-feature AI app that combines at least two AI capabilities into one coherent product.

2. Task 2: Show how your app separates the interface, AI logic, and data layers for maintainability.

3. Task 3: Add a third feature that reuses an existing AI building block and integrate it cleanly.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is a multi-feature AI application?**

**Answer:** An app that combines several AI capabilities, such as summarisation, Q\&A, and generation, into one coherent tool.

**Q2. How should multi-feature design start?**

**Answer:** From the user's needs, organising features logically with a shared data layer and consistent interface.

**Q3. What does assembly require?**

**Answer:** Composing features so they work together smoothly, share data appropriately, and present a unified experience.

**Q4. How is a multi-feature app kept maintainable?**

**Answer:** By cleanly separating the interface, the AI logic, and the data layers.

**Q5. What does building such an app demonstrate?**

**Answer:** The step from single-purpose tools to genuine products with real depth that people will use.

**LAB 49**  
**Week 17  |  Module 8: Autonomous AI Agents**

**AI Agent Fundamentals: Reasoning, Tools & the Agent Loop**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Explain what AI agents are and how they work, covering reasoning, tool use, and the core agent loop.

**▍ Theory / Background**

AI agents represent a shift from AI that responds to AI that acts. An agent is an AI system that can pursue a goal by reasoning about what to do, taking actions using tools, observing the results, and iterating until the goal is achieved. This autonomy distinguishes agents from simple chatbots.

The core of an agent is the agent loop: think, act, observe, repeat. Given a goal, the agent reasons about the next step (think), performs an action such as calling a tool or API (act), receives the outcome (observe), and uses it to decide the next step — looping until done. This cycle enables multi-step problem solving.

Tools are what give agents their power. A tool is a capability the agent can invoke — a web search, a calculator, a database query, a code executor. The agent decides when and how to use each tool based on the task. Tools let the agent affect the world and gather information beyond its training.

Reasoning ties it together. The agent uses the LLM's reasoning to plan, choose tools, interpret results, and recover from errors. Frameworks like ReAct (Reason \+ Act) formalise this interleaving of thought and action. Understanding the loop, tools, and reasoning is the foundation for building agents.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Agent loop: think-act-observe |
| **Deliverable** | An agent-loop trace |
| **Framework** | ReAct (Reason \+ Act) |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Define what distinguishes an agent from a chatbot.

2. Describe the think-act-observe loop.

3. List example tools an agent could use.

4. Trace a simple goal through the agent loop on paper.

5. Show where reasoning chooses each action.

6. Note how observations feed the next step.

7. Summarise the ReAct pattern.

**▍ Agent Loop Trace (Colab)**

| \# AI Agent Fundamentals: the agent loop (Colab)   \# An AGENT pursues a goal by looping: THINK \-\> ACT \-\> OBSERVE.   GOAL \= "What is the population of Pakistan times 2?"   \# \--- ReAct-style trace (Reason \+ Act) \--- trace \= """ Thought: I need the population of Pakistan, then multiply by 2\. Action: web\_search("current population of Pakistan") Observation: \~241 million. Thought: Now multiply 241,000,000 by 2\. Action: calculator("241000000 \* 2") Observation: 482,000,000. Thought: I now have the final answer. Answer: About 482 million. """ print(trace)   \# \--- Tools the agent can call \--- TOOLS \= {     "web\_search": "find current information",     "calculator": "do exact maths",     "database":   "query stored records",     "code\_exec":  "run code", } for name, desc in TOOLS.items():     print(f"{name:\<12} \-\> {desc}")   print("\\nLoop: think \-\> act (use a tool) \-\> observe \-\> repeat until done.") |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A traced example of the agent loop (think-act-observe) solving a multi-step goal using tools, with the reasoning choosing each action and observations feeding the next step, illustrating the ReAct pattern.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Trace a multi-step goal through the agent loop, showing the think-act-observe cycle and tool use at each step.

2. Task 2: List four tools an agent could use and a task each would help with.

3. Task 3: Explain how an agent differs from a simple chatbot in your own words.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What is an AI agent?**

**Answer:** An AI system that pursues a goal by reasoning, taking actions with tools, observing results, and iterating until done.

**Q2. What is the agent loop?**

**Answer:** Think, act, observe, repeat — reasoning about the next step, taking an action, observing the outcome, and continuing.

**Q3. What is a tool in an agent context?**

**Answer:** A capability the agent can invoke, such as web search, a calculator, a database query, or code execution.

**Q4. What is the ReAct pattern?**

**Answer:** A framework interleaving reasoning (thought) and action (tool use) so the agent plans and acts step by step.

**Q5. How do agents differ from chatbots?**

**Answer:** Chatbots respond to prompts, while agents autonomously plan and take multi-step actions to achieve goals.

**LAB 50**  
**Week 17  |  Module 8: Autonomous AI Agents**

**Building Your First Tool-Using Agent**

Student Name: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Roll No: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Date: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Marks: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

Instructor: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_Signature: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**▍ Objective**

Build a simple agent that can use one or more tools to accomplish a task it could not do with text alone.

**▍ Theory / Background**

This session moves from understanding agents to building one. A first tool-using agent connects an LLM to a tool — such as a calculator or search function — and lets the model decide when to use it. This demonstrates the essential agent mechanism: the model choosing and invoking actions.

The mechanism works by describing the available tools to the model and instructing it to respond either with an answer or with a tool call. When the model requests a tool, your code executes it and feeds the result back. The model then continues, using the result to reach the final answer.

Modern AI APIs support this through function calling (or tool use): you define functions with their parameters, the model returns a structured request to call one, your code runs it, and the conversation continues with the result. This is the production-standard way to build tool-using agents.

Even a single-tool agent is powerful. An LLM that cannot reliably do arithmetic becomes accurate when given a calculator tool; one that lacks current data becomes informed with a search tool. Building this teaches the core pattern that scales to sophisticated multi-tool agents.

**▍ Tools & Requirements**

| Platform | Google Colab |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Concept** | Function calling / tool use |
| **Deliverable** | A working single-tool agent |
| **Skill** | Let the model invoke a tool |

**▍ Step-by-Step Procedure**

1. Define a tool as a Python function (e.g. a calculator).

2. Describe the tool to the model (name, purpose, parameters).

3. Instruct the model to call the tool when needed.

4. Detect when the model requests the tool.

5. Execute the tool and return the result to the model.

6. Let the model produce the final answer using the result.

7. Test on a task the model cannot do alone.

**▍ Python Code (Colab)**

| \# First Tool-Using Agent (Colab) import json   \# \--- 1\. Define a tool \--- def calculator(expression):     return eval(expression)        \# in real use, sandbox this\!   TOOLS \= {"calculator": calculator}   \# \--- 2\. Describe the tool to the model (function calling) \--- tool\_spec \= \[{     "name": "calculator",     "description": "Evaluate a maths expression",     "parameters": {"type": "object",                    "properties": {"expression": {"type": "string"}}}, }\]   \# \--- 3\. The agent loop \--- def run\_agent(question):     \# resp \= call\_ai(question, tools=tool\_spec)     \# The model returns EITHER an answer OR a tool call request:     tool\_request \= {"tool": "calculator", "args": {"expression": "1234 \* 5678"}}       if tool\_request:         \# 4\. Execute the requested tool         fn \= TOOLS\[tool\_request\["tool"\]\]         result \= fn(\*\*tool\_request\["args"\])         \# 5\. Feed result back \-\> model gives final answer         \# final \= call\_ai(f"Tool result: {result}. Answer the question.")         return f"The answer is {result}."   print(run\_agent("What is 1234 times 5678?")) \# The LLM alone is unreliable at big multiplication; \# with the calculator tool it is exact. |
| :---- |

**▍ Expected Output**

A working single-tool agent that describes a tool to the model, detects when the model requests it, executes the tool, feeds the result back, and produces an accurate final answer on a task the model could not do alone.

**▍ Practice Tasks**

1. Task 1: Build a single-tool agent (e.g. with a calculator) and demonstrate it succeeding on a task the LLM fails alone.

2. Task 2: Explain how function calling lets the model request a tool and how your code responds.

3. Task 3: Add input safety to your tool so it cannot execute dangerous operations.

**▍ Viva Questions & Answers**

**Q1. What does a first tool-using agent do?**

**Answer:** It connects an LLM to a tool and lets the model decide when to use it, demonstrating the model choosing actions.

**Q2. How does the tool-use mechanism work?**

**Answer:** The tools are described to the model, which requests a tool call; your code executes it and feeds the result back.

**Q3. What is function calling?**

**Answer:** An AI API feature where you define functions and the model returns a structured request to call one, which your code runs.

**Q4. Why is even a single-tool agent powerful?**

**Answer:** Because a tool like a calculator or search makes the LLM accurate or informed where it was unreliable alone.

**Q5. What pattern does building this teach?**

**Answer:** The core agent pattern of the model choosing and invoking actions, which scales to multi-tool agents.
